Semantic Bounds and Strong Call-by-Value Normalization
Beniamino Accattoli, Giulio Guerrieri, Maico Leberle

Abstract
This paper explores two topics at once: the use of denotational semantics to bound the evaluation length of functional programs, and the semantics of strong (that is, possibly under abstractions) call-by-value evaluation. About the first, we analyze de Carvalho’s seminal use of relational semantics for bounding the evaluation length of λ \lambda -terms, starting from the presentation of the semantics as an intersection types system. We focus on the part of his work which is usually neglected in its many recent adaptations, despite being probably the conceptually deeper one: how to transfer the bounding power from the type system to the relational semantics itself. We dissect this result and re-understand it via the isolation of a simpler size representation property . About the second, we use relational semantics to develop a semantical study of strong call-by-value evaluation, which is both a delicate and neglected topic. We give a semantic characterization of terms normalizable with respect to strong evaluation, providing in particular the first result of adequacy with respect to strong call-by-value. Moreover, we extract bounds about strong evaluation from both the type systems and the relational semantics. Essentially, we use strong call-by-value to revisit de Carvalho’s semantic bounds, and de Carvalho’s technique to provide semantical foundations for strong call-by-value.



I
Introduction

In this paper we are concerned with two topics of the theory of
\lambda
-calculus, in which linear logic plays a prominent role. The first one is the use of denotational semantics to obtain bounds on operational aspects of
\lambda
-terms. The second one is the semantics of call-by-value (shortened to CbV) evaluation.

Both these topics have a literature of their own. For different reasons, and in different ways, however, neither of them has found clean and solid grounds. We aim to use the two topics, together with linear logic technology, to clarify each other.

I-A

De Carvalho’s Semantical Bounds

Denotational semantics studies invariants of evaluation. The typical way in which it is connected to the operational semantics of
\lambda
-calculi is at the
qualitative
level, via
adequacy
: the denotational interpretation
\llbracket t\rrbracket
of a
\lambda
-term
t
is non-trivial (typically non-empty) if and only if the evaluation of
t
terminates.

At first sight, denotational semantics cannot provide
quantitative
operational insights such as evaluation lengths, because of its invariance by evaluation. The question is in fact subtler. Being invariant by evaluation, denotational semantics models normal forms,
and in a
compositional
way: by composing the interpretations of two terms one can obtain the interpretation of the result of their application—therefore, denotational semantics does reflect the evaluation process
somehow
.

This intuition guided de Carvalho in its use of linear logic relational semantics—probably the simplest denotational model of linear logic—to not only characterize semantically termination, but also obtain exact bounds on the evaluation lengths of
\lambda
-terms
[
19
,
20
]
.
A key aspect of his work is that he uses a type system to give a syntactic presentation of relational semantics. The types are the
non-idempotent
variant of intersection types, also known as
multi types
, because non-idempotent intersections are multi sets. The interpretation
\llbracket t\rrbracket
of a
\lambda
-term
t
in relational semantics is given by the types (including the typing context
\Gamma
) in all the judgments
\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}
that can be derived for
t
. It is important to stress that relational semantics can also be presented independently of multi types.

Three Kinds of Bounds

In
[
19
,
20
]
, de Carvalho measures two forms of strong evaluations realized by abstract machines implementing the head and leftmost call-by-name strategies, organizing his results in two parts. In this overview, we prefer to slightly depart from the details of his work, forgetting about abstract machines, focussing on leftmost evaluation, and isolating three (rather than two) kinds of bounds:

1.

Bounds from type derivations
: given a type derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}
, he shows that the size
|\Phi|
of the derivation bounds the number of leftmost steps from
t
to its normal form
\mathsf{nf}{(t)}

plus
the size
|\mathsf{nf}{(t)}|
of the normal form. Moreover, minimal derivations provide exact bounds.

2.

Size bounds from types
: the types in the final judgment—a point of the relational interpretation
\llbracket t\rrbracket
—also provide a bound, independently of the derivation
\Phi
. Being invariant by evaluation, they cannot bound evaluation lengths. They do however bound the size
|\mathsf{nf}{(t)}|
of the normal form, and bounds are exact when types are minimal.

3.

Bounds from composable types
: de Carvalho shows that types can be used to bound evaluation lengths
compositionally
,
i.e.
, from the types in
\llbracket t\rrbracket
and
\llbracket u\rrbracket
it is possible to extract bounds about the leftmost evaluation and the normal form of
tu
, with
no reference to type derivations
.

The third kind is where the bounding power of the type system (just one possible way of defining relational semantics) is fully
lifted
to relational semantics. Therefore, the lifting guarantees that the bounding power is an inherent feature of the relational model—multi types are just a handy tool to show it.

Of the three results, the third one is also the more technical one. In particular, it requires to enrich the type system with an infinity of ground types and work up to type substitutions. The somewhat puzzling fact is that these extra features play no role in the two previous points.

De Carvalho’s Legacy

De Carvalho developed his results in his PhD defended in 2007
[
19
]
, known by the community thanks to a technical report that was eventually published much later
[
20
]
.
Soon after his PhD, he adapted his work to linear logic, with Pagani and Tortora de Falco
[
21
,
22
]
. A few years later, Bernadet and Graham-Lengrand adapted his work to measure the longest evaluation in the
\lambda
-calculus
[
14
]
, but they only provided bounds of kind 1 (
from type derivations
).

At the time, it was not known whether it would make sense to count the number of
\beta
-steps (or linear logic cut-elimination steps) as a reasonable measure of complexity. After this was clarified (in the positive) by Accattoli and Dal Lago
[
4
]
, de Carvalho’s work has been revisited by Accattoli, Graham-Lengrand, and Kesner in 2018
[
6
]
. The revisitation started a new wave of works adapting de Carvalho’s study to many evaluation strategies and extensions of the
\lambda
-calculus, including call-by-value
[
8
]
, call-by-need
[
9
]
, a linear logic presentation of call-by-push-value
[
15
]
, the
\lambda\mu
-calculus
[
35
]
, the
\lambda
-calculus with pattern matching
[
12
]
, the probabilistic
\lambda
-calculus
[
18
]
, and the abstract machine underlying the geometry of interaction
[
5
]
. All these works provide bounds of kind 1, and some of them also of kind 2, but none of them deals with those of kind 3 (
bounds from composable types
).

Contributions of This Paper for Semantic Bounds

We adapt de Carvalho’s study to another setting, Strong CbV (surveyed below). Contrary to the new wave of similar works, we provide all three kinds of bounds, including
bounds from composable types
, appeared only in
[
20
,
21
]
. Beyond proving the results, we have a very close look at this part, isolating the subtleties and decomposing
it in smaller steps. In particular:

•

Subtlety 1, minimality does not work
: when bounding a single term, both derivations and types provide exact bounds when they are minimal. When dealing with the application of
t
to
u
, every pair of composable types for them provides bounds. The minimal composable pair, however, does not provide exact bounds.

•

Subtlety 2, the gap between derivations and types
: the previous subtlety stems from the fact that, for a normal term
t
, both the derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}
and the types in
\Gamma
and
{\mathsf{A}}
provide bounds for
|t|
, but they may not coincide. In general, the bound from types is laxer. The bounds gap hinders the possibility of lifting the bounding power from derivations to types, if bounds from some derivations are not reflected by any type in the interpretation
\llbracket t\rrbracket
.

•

Size representation property
: luckily, given
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}
for which there is a gap between the bound from
\Phi
and the bound from
\Gamma
and
{\mathsf{A}}
, there always is a second derivation
\Psi\vartriangleright\Delta\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{B}}
, whose types
\Delta
and
{\mathsf{B}}
give the same bound as the first derivation
\Phi
. Then, all bounds coming from derivations can also be seen as coming from types, potentially from the types of other derivations.

Size representation is the key property where the lifting of the bounding power from type derivations to types—and thus to relational semantics—takes place. On the good side, it does not need an infinity of ground types nor type substitutions, so it is simpler than what done in
[
20
,
21
]
. On the bad side, it only implies a weaker form of de Carvalho’s final results.

We then show that, by enriching the type system as de Carvalho does, one can reinforce the size representation property into a
dissection property
describing how to obtain
\Phi
from
\Psi
via a type substitution. Finally, the dissection property implies de Carvalho’s
bounds from composable types
.

The refinement, however, is of a technical nature, and does not rest nor introduce any deep concept. Therefore, we claim that the key point in lifting the bounds from the type system to the relational model is the size representation property.

I-B

The Semantics of Strong Call-by-Value

Plotkin’s call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus
[
44
]
is at
the heart of
programming languages such as OCaml and proof assistants such as Coq. In the study of programming
languages, call-by-value (CbV) evaluation is usually
weak
, that is, it does not reduce under
abstractions, and terms are assumed to be
closed
,
i.e.
, without free variables.
These constraints give rise to an elegant framework—we call it
Closed CbV
, following

[
7
]
.

It often happens, however, that one needs to go beyond Closed CbV by
considering
Strong CbV
, which is the extended setting where reduction under abstractions is allowed
and terms may be open, or the intermediate framework of
Open CbV
, where evaluation is weak
but terms are not necessarily closed.
The need arises, most notably, when describing the implementation model of Coq, as done by Grégoire and Leroy

[
28
]
, to realize the essential conversion test for dependent types. Other
motivations lie in the study of bisimulations by Lassen

[
37
]
, partial evaluation
[
34
]
, or various topics
of a semantical or logical nature, recalled below.

Naïve Extension of CbV.
In call-by-name (CbN) turning to open terms or strong
evaluation is harmless because CbN does not impose any special form to the arguments of

\beta
-redexes.
On the contrary, turning to Open or Strong CbV is delicate. While some fundamental properties such
as confluence and standardization hold also in such cases, as showed by Plotkin’s himself
[
44
]
,
others—typically of a semantical nature—break as soon as one considers open terms.

The problems of Strong CbV can be traced back to Plotkin’s seminal paper,
where he points out the
incompleteness of CbV with respect to CPS translations, an issue later solved with categorical
tools by Moggi
[
41
]
. This led to a number of studies, among others

[
47
,
48
,
39
,
17
,
23
,
32
]
, that
introduced many proposals of
improved calculi for CbV.

The relationship with denotational semantics is also problematic, as first shown by Paolini and Ronchi della Rocca

[
43
,
42
,
46
]
. There are two subtle
points:

1.

Solvability
: the adaptation of the notion of solvability—roughly, a form of
meaningfulness for terms—to CbV;

2.

Adequacy
: denotational semantics that are

adequate
for Closed CbV
[
1
,
24
,
33
,
45
]
are no longer adequate for the extended settings. Roughly,
there are terms that are semantically divergent, that is, with trivial semantics (or unsolvable),
while they are normal forms with respect to Plotkin’s rules, and so are expected to have non-trivial

Let us stress that, for a CbV model, adequacy
1
1

1

In the literature,
e.g.

[
38
]
, the word
adequacy
can have different meanings.
is somewhat mandatory, because any model of CbN provides a non-adequate model of CbV that does not model the CbV behavior.

The problem with adequacy can be seen also from a logical point of view. The terms pointed out by Paolini and Ronchi Della Rocca diverge also if seen as
linear logic proof nets, as pointed out by Accattoli

[
2
]
, or as terms in the computational interpretation of sequent
calculus by Curien and Herbelin
[
17
]
.

Linear Logic and the Semantical Issues of Open/Strong CbV

Both semantical issues of Open/Strong CbV have been addressed in the literature, relying of linear logic tools.

About solvability, Accattoli and Paolini
[
10
]
characterize operationally solvable terms using a calculus isomorphic to the proof-net CbV representation of
\lambda
-calculus, the
value substitution calculus
(shortened to VSC).
Namely, they introduce a
solvable evaluation strategy
(called stratified-weak in
[
10
]
) in the VSC that terminates if and only if the term is solvable.
This is akin to what happens in CbN, where solvable term are those for which head evaluation terminates; such a characterization is impossible in Plotkin’s original formulation of CbV.

About adequacy, Accattoli and Guerrieri
[
8
]
showed that (a calculus equivalent to) the
open
sub-calculus of the VSC is adequate with respect to Ehrhard’s CbV relational semantics
[
25
]
, the CbV analogous of the system used by de Carvalho. Moreover, they adapted de Carvalho’s quantitative bounds (namely, of kind 1 and 2) to Open CbV.

Asymmetry Between CbN and CbV.
The described results—the solvable strategy characterizing CbV solvability, and adequacy of relational semantics for Open CbV—show that a semantical theory of CbV beyond the closed case is possible. Much still needs to be done, though, to have a solid theory and to close the gap with the very well studied CbN case.

In particular, concerning adequacy one would expect a similar result also for evaluation in Strong CbV, as it is the case for strong CbN—there are however no such results in the literature. Even worse about the quantitative refinement: while there are strategies in the literature that are fully normalizing for Strong CbV—essentially the leftmost-outermost strategy—they are formulated in calculi
for Strong CbV (
e.g.

[
29
,
31
,
26
]
)
whose number of steps cannot be measured via multi types nor can be taken as a reasonable time cost model (see below).

Subtleties of Strong CbV

Let us clarify a basic point of Strong CbV. A
value
is a
variable or an abstraction. In CbV,
\beta
-redexes can be fired only when the argument is a value (and so only values can be duplicated or erased).
In Closed CbV, values are normal forms—it can be thought as a
call-by-normal-form
calculus.
A
similar property can be recovered also in Open CbV (by slightly extending the notion of value), see
[
7
]
.
In Strong CbV such an essence is lost. To give an idea, if
\Omega
denotes
the usual diverging term, then in Strong CbV
\lambda x..\Omega
diverges (evaluation under abstraction) while

(\lambda y.z)(\lambda x..\Omega)
may both diverge and reduce to
z
—the argument need not be fully normalized, but
only reduced to a (weak) value.
This is mandatory, to be conservative over Closed and Open CbV.
Thus, there is an inherent underlying tension in Strong CbV: on the one hand, values keep
playing an essential role to identify which
\beta
-redexes can be fired, on the other hand they no longer
suffice to describe evaluation and normal forms, that become more sophisticated notions.

Contributions of This Paper for the Semantics of Strong CbV

We provide a quantitative study of Strong CbV via multi types. Our contributions are:

•

Normalizing strong strategy
: we define a strong VSC strategy generalizing leftmost-outermost evaluation, named
external strategy
, and prove it normalizing, that is, it reaches a VSC normal form whenever there is one.

•

Normalization, multi types, and adequacy
: we give a characterization of normalizable terms via multi types, obtaining the adequacy theorem.

•

Semantical bounds
: we show the three kinds of de Carvalho’s bounds with respect to the external strategy. In particular, we dissect the
bounds from composable types
kind, as explained in the first part of the introduction.

Reasonable Cost Models

In a companion paper by Accattoli, Condoluci, and Sacerdoti Coen
[
LICS2021machine
]
, it is proved that the number of steps of the external strategy is a reasonable time cost model for Strong CbV (where
reasonable
means polynomially related to the time cost model of Turing machines). The proof is based on a novel abstract machine that implements the external strategy within a overhead which is bilinear (that is, linear in the number of
\beta
/multiplicative steps and in the size of the initial term). When combined with the study of this paper, we obtain that multi types provide quantitative bounds that are meaningful from a computational complexity point of view.

Syntactic Variants

We study Strong CbV via the VSC, to stress the linear logic background and foundation of our study. Everything could equivalently be easily reformulated inside (the intuitionistic and CbV fragment of) Curien and Herbelin’s

\overline{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu}
-calculus, following the isomorphism with the VSC developed in
[
7
]

and preserving the number of steps/cost model. The external strategy can also be reformulated using the
shuffling calculus

\lambda_{\mathsf{shuf}}
by Guerrieri and Carraro

[
16
]
, a calculus with commuting conversions used recently also by Manzonetto, Pagani, and Ronchi della Rocca

[
38
]
. The cost model of
\lambda_{\mathsf{shuf}}
, however, is unclear (see

[
7
]
) and commuting conversion steps cannot be counted via multi
types (but they should)—
\lambda_{\mathsf{shuf}}
provides a qualitative foundation for Strong CbV, but it cannot be used for a
quantitative one. Calculi with
\mathsf{let}
-commutation rules such as the one by Herbelin and Zimmerman
[
32
]
simply
can be seen as subcalculi of the VSC (up to structural equivalence, see
[
10
]
). Similar remarks apply to many other CbV calculi

[
41
,
47
,
48
,
39
,
23
,
26
]
. The key point of the VSC (valid also
in
\overline{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu}
) is that it does not need any commuting rule.

Summing Up

We study Strong CbV using a calculus, the VSC, rooted in linear logic. We provide a solid foundation of Strong CbV with respect to multi types and relational semantics, analogous to the one for CbN, by characterizing qualitatively and quantitatively Strong CbV normalization. Moreover, we fully express the quantitative aspect of relational semantics by adapting de Carvalho’s semantic bounds and re-understanding them via the isolation of the key
size representation
property.

Proofs

Proofs are in the Appendix.

II
Value Substitution Calculus

Here we present the
value substitution calculus
(VSC for short) introduced by Accattoli and Paolini
[
10
]
, and we recall some properties.
The VSC is a
\lambda
-calculus with let-expressions whose reduction rules mimic cut-elimination on proof-nets, via Girard’s CbV translation
(A\Rightarrow B)^{v}=\oc(A^{v}\multimap B^{v})
of intuitionistic logic into linear logic, as explained in
[
2
]
.

In VSC,
\beta
-redexes are decomposed via let-expressions, and the

by-value
restriction on evaluation is on the let-substitution rule, not on
\beta
-redexes, because only values
can be substituted.
A let-expression is formulated as an
explicit substitution
or
sharing
(
ES
for short)
t[x{\shortleftarrow}u]
which binds
x
in
t
.
All along the
paper we use (many notions of)
contexts
,
i.e.
terms with a hole, noted
\langle\cdot\rangle
. For now, we need
substitution contexts

L
, which are simply lists of
ES
. The grammars are:

\begin{array}[]{rrl}\textsc{Values }&v&\Coloneqq\lambda x.t\\
\textsc{Terms }&t,u,s&\Coloneqq x\mid v\mid tu\mid t[x{\shortleftarrow}u]\\
\textsc{Substitution Ctxs }&L&\Coloneqq\langle\cdot\rangle\mid L[x{\shortleftarrow}t]\end{array}

The set of free variables of term
t
is denoted by
{\sf fv}(t)
.
Plugging a term
t
in a context
C
is noted
C\langle t\rangle
, possibly capturing variables.
An
answer
is a term of the shape
L\langle v\rangle
, where
v
is a value (
i.e.
an abstraction) and
L
is a substitution context.
We use
t\{x{\shortleftarrow}u\}
for the capture-avoiding substitution of

t
for each free occurrence of
x
in
t
.
There are two kinds of rewrite rules, both work
at a distance
, that is, up to a substitution context.

\begin{array}[]{rr@{\ }l@{\ }l}\textsc{Multiplicative rule}&L\langle\lambda x.t\rangle u&\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}&L\langle t[x{\shortleftarrow}u]\rangle\\
\textsc{Exponential rule}&t[x{\shortleftarrow}L\langle v\rangle]&\mapsto_{{\mathsf{e}}}&L\langle t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v\}\rangle\end{array}

We shall consider three fragments of the VSC.
They all contain the terms of VSC, they differ only in the choice of evaluation contexts for the rewrite rules.

The Open VSC

We first focus on the open fragment of the VSC, where rewriting is forbidden under abstraction and terms are possibly open (but not necessarily). This fragment has a nice inductive description of its normal forms, called fireballs, that is the starting point for many other definitions in the paper. Open contexts and rules are defined as follows.

\begin{aligned} \textsc{Open ctxs}&&O&\Coloneqq\langle\cdot\rangle\mid Ot\mid tO\mid O[x{\shortleftarrow}t]\mid t[x{\shortleftarrow}O]\end{aligned}

Open rewrite rules
:

t\mapsto_{a}t^{\prime}
O\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}a}O\langle t^{\prime}\rangle

(
a\in\{\mathsf{m},{\mathsf{e}}\}
)

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\textsc{Open reduction}:&\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}\,\coloneqq\,\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}\end{array}

Proposition II.1

(Properties of the open reduction)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
B.2

1.

\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
is diamond;
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
strongly commute.

2.

A term is
{\mathsf{o}}
-normal if and only if it is a fireball, where
fireballs
(and
inert terms
) are defined by:

\begin{array}[]{r r@{\ } l}\textsc{Inert term}&i&\Coloneqq x\mid if\mid i[x{\shortleftarrow}i^{\prime}]\\
\textsc{Fireball}&f&\Coloneqq v\mid i\mid f[x{\shortleftarrow}i]\end{array}

Diamond of
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
and strong commutation of
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
are technical facts (their definitions are in
Appendix

A
in the Appendix) with relevant consequences:

\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
is confluent and its non-determinism is only apparent, because if an
{\mathsf{o}}
-evaluation from
t
reaches
a
{\mathsf{o}}
-normal form
u
, then
every

{\mathsf{o}}
-evaluation from
t
eventually ends in
u
. And all these
{\mathsf{o}}
-evaluations have the same length and same number of
\mathsf{m}
-steps and
{\mathsf{e}}
-steps.
Same properties hold for other strategies we shall consider.

For our quantitative study we need a notion of size specific for every fragment of the VSC. The
open size

|t|_{{\mathsf{o}}}
of a term
t
is its number of applications out of abstractions,
i.e.

\displaystyle|x|_{{\mathsf{o}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 0

\displaystyle|tu|_{{\mathsf{o}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|_{{\mathsf{o}}}+|u|_{{\mathsf{o}}}+1

\displaystyle|\lambda x.{t}|_{{\mathsf{o}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 0

\displaystyle|t[x{\shortleftarrow}u]|_{{\mathsf{o}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|_{{\mathsf{o}}}+|u|_{{\mathsf{o}}}.

The
Closed
VSC

is the restriction of the Open VSC to closed terms. Its normal forms are the closed values.

The Strong VSC

The
Strong
VSC is obtained by allowing rewriting rules everywhere.

\begin{array}[]{l r rclccc}\textsc{\text{Strong} ctxs}&F\Coloneqq\langle\cdot\rangle\mid Ft\mid tF\mid\lambda x.{F}\mid F[x{\shortleftarrow}t]\mid t[x{\shortleftarrow}F]\end{array}

Strong
rewrite rules:

t\mapsto_{a}t^{\prime}
F\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{a}F\langle t^{\prime}\rangle

(
a\in\{\mathsf{m},{\mathsf{e}}\}
)

\begin{array}[]{r l@{\ } l@{\ } lllllll}\textsc{\text{Strong} reduction:}&\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}&\coloneqq&\rightarrow_{\mathsf{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\end{array}

Unlike the previous cases,
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
is not diamond: consider all the
\mathsf{vsc}
-evaluations of
(xx)[x{\shortleftarrow}\lambda y.{\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}}]
, with
{\mathsf{I}}\coloneqq\lambda z.z
.

Proposition II.2

(Properties of the
strong
reduction)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
B.5

1.

The reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
is confluent.

2.

A term is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal if and only if it is a
strong
fireball, where
strong
fireballs
(and
strong
inert terms
,
strong
values
) are:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\textsc{\text{Strong} inert terms }&i_{\mathsf{s}}\Coloneqq x\mid i_{\mathsf{s}}f_{\mathsf{s}}\mid i_{\mathsf{s}}[x{\shortleftarrow}i^{\prime}_{\mathsf{s}}]\\
\textsc{\text{Strong} values }&v_{\mathsf{s}}\Coloneqq\lambda x.f_{\mathsf{s}}\\
\textsc{\text{Strong} fireballs }&f_{\mathsf{s}}\Coloneqq i_{\mathsf{s}}\mid v_{\mathsf{s}}\mid f_{\mathsf{s}}[x{\shortleftarrow}i_{\mathsf{s}}]\end{array}

The notions of
strong
inert terms and
strong
fireballs are a generalization of inert terms and fireballs, respectively, by simply iterating the construction under all abstractions.
Note that they are similar to normal forms of the (CbN)
\lambda
-calculus, but they can have
ES
containing
strong
inert terms.

The
strong
size

|t|_{{\mathsf{s}}}
—or simply size—of a term
t
is its number of applications and abstractions,
i.e.

\displaystyle|x|_{{\mathsf{s}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 0

\displaystyle|tu|_{{\mathsf{s}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|_{{\mathsf{s}}}+|u|_{{\mathsf{s}}}+1

\displaystyle|\lambda x.{t}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|_{{\mathsf{s}}}+1

\displaystyle|t[x{\shortleftarrow}u]|_{{\mathsf{s}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|_{{\mathsf{s}}}+|u|_{{\mathsf{s}}}.

On the Value of Variables

In Plotkin’s original work
[
44
]
, and in works of a theoretical nature, values are abstractions or variables.
In implementative works, variables are excluded from values.
The difference is not only terminological, but also semantic because it affects whether exponential step can fire
t[x{\shortleftarrow}y]
or not.
Accattoli and Sacerdoti Coen
[
11
]
showed that excluding variables from values brings a speed up in implementations, which explains the discrepancy.
Actually, from a theoretical viewpoint, variables have a double nature as both values and (
strong
) inert terms, in that all the statements concerning types for values hold with or without variables and the same for statements concerning types for inert terms.
We chose to define values as only abstractions and to include variables in the (
strong
) inert terms essentially for two reasons.

First, for (
strong
) inert terms we shall prove stronger claims about typability to have the right inductive hypothesis.
So, seeing variables as (
strong
) inert terms is somehow
more general
.

Second, considering variables as values so that exponential steps can also fire
ES
with variables is
irrelevant
with respect to evaluation.
Let us see that more in detail.
We set:

\begin{array}[]{l r rclccc}&\!\!t[x{\shortleftarrow}L\langle y\rangle]\mapsto_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}\!L\langle t\{x{\shortleftarrow}y\}\rangle&\ {\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 15.87509pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$t\mapsto_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}t^{\prime}$}}\hbox{$F\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}\!F\langle t^{\prime}\rangle$}}\par}\par}}}$}}\end{array}

Proposition II.3

(Irrelevance of
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
B.9

1.

Postponement
: whenever
d\colon t\,(\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}})^{*}\,u
then there is
d^{\prime}\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}\!\cdot\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}\,u
with
|d^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|d|_{\mathsf{m}}
;

2.

Termination
: a term is weakly (
resp.
strongly) normalizing for
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
if and only if so is it for
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\!\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
.

Plotkin
vs
VSC

Plotkin’s original CbV
\lambda
-calculus
\lambda_{\mathsf{Plot}}

[
44
]
can be easily simulated in the VSC.
The syntax of
\lambda_{\mathsf{Plot}}
is simply the same as in the VSC but without
ES
.
The reduction in
\lambda_{\mathsf{Plot}}
is the closure under
strong
contexts (without
ES
) of the rule
(\lambda x.t)v_{\mathsf{t}}\mapsto_{\beta_{v}}t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}
,
where
v_{\mathsf{t}}
is a
theoretical value
,
i.e.
a variable or an abstraction.
Since
\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}
fires also variables, the simulation requires also
{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}
-steps, but we have seen that this extension is harmless in VSC (
Proposition

II.3
).

Proposition II.4

(Simulation)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
B.10

Let
t
be a term without
ES
.

1.

If
t\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}t^{\prime}
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{m}}\!\cdot\,(\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}})\ t^{\prime}
.

2.

If
t\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}^{*}t^{\prime}
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}u\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}t^{\prime}
for some term
u
.

There is no sensible way to simulate VSC into
\lambda_{\mathsf{Plot}}
.
Indeed VSC is a proper extension of
\lambda_{\mathsf{Plot}}
: VSC makes divergent terms such as
(\lambda x.\delta)(yy)\delta
and
\delta((\lambda x.\delta)(yy))
that are
\beta_{v}
-normal.

III
The External Strategy

Here we define the
strong
strategy, called
external
, that we shall show to be
\mathsf{vsc}
-normalizing, as a corollary of our type theoretical study.
Its role is analogous to the leftmost-outermost
strategy of the
\lambda
-calculus. A notable difference, however, is that the external strategy is itself non-deterministic, but in a
harmless way, as it is diamond.

We need a few notions. First,
rigid terms
,
i.e.
the variation over
strong
inert terms where the arguments of the head variable can be whatever term (note that
answers
are not rigid terms).

\textsc{Rigid terms}\quad r,r^{\prime}\Coloneqq x\mid rt\mid r[x{\shortleftarrow}r^{\prime}]

Every (
strong
) inert term is a rigid term, but the converse does not hold—consider
y({\delta{\mathsf{I}}})
where
\delta\coloneqq\lambda x.{xx}
and
{\mathsf{I}}=\lambda z.{z}
.

Then we need evaluation contexts for the external strategy
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
. The base case is given by the open rewriting rules (themselves defined via a closure by open contexts, see
Section

II
), which are then closed by
external (evaluation) contexts
, defined by mutual induction with
rigid contexts
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad} r@{\ } llcc}\textsc{External ctxs}&X&\Coloneqq\langle\cdot\rangle\mid\lambda x.X\mid t[x{\shortleftarrow}R]\mid X[x{\shortleftarrow}r]\mid R\\
\textsc{Rigid ctxs}&R&\Coloneqq rX\mid Rt\mid R[x{\shortleftarrow}r]\mid r[x{\shortleftarrow}R]\end{array}

External rewrite rules:

t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}a}t^{\prime}
X\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}a}X\langle t^{\prime}\rangle

(
a\in\{\mathsf{m},{\mathsf{e}}\}
)

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\textsc{External reduction:}&\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}\,\coloneqq\,\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}\end{array}

The strategy diverges on
y(\lambda z.\delta\delta)
and normalizes the potentially diverging term
(\lambda x.y)(\lambda z.\delta\delta)\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}^{*}y
.
The grammars of external and rigid contexts allow evaluation
to enter only inside non-applied abstractions,
e.g.

(\lambda x.({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}}))v\not\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}(\lambda x.(y[y{\shortleftarrow}{\mathsf{I}}]))v
.
This is a sort of outside-in order
2
2

2

More precisely, the strategy
respects the box order of
[
3
]
, but according to how boxes are used by the CbV translation into linear logic, which is different from the one underlying
[
3
]
. Also,
the box order is incomparable with respect to the
least level
order of the linear logic literature.

which
is neither left-to-right nor right-to-left—we have both
({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}})({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}})\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}(y[y{\shortleftarrow}{\mathsf{I}}])({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}})

and
({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}})({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}})\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}})(y[y{\shortleftarrow}{\mathsf{I}}])
—since open contexts do not impose an order on applications. As just showed the strategy is non-deterministic: another example is given by
t=x(\lambda y.({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}}))[x{\shortleftarrow}x({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}})]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}x(\lambda y.z[z{\shortleftarrow}{\mathsf{I}}])[x{\shortleftarrow}x({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}})]
, and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}x(\lambda y.({\mathsf{I}}{\mathsf{I}}))[x{\shortleftarrow}x(z[z{\shortleftarrow}{\mathsf{I}}])]
.
Such a behavior however is only a relaxed form of determinism, as it satisfies the
diamond
property
(see also the comment after
Proposition

II.1
).

Proposition III.1

(Properties of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
C.3

Let
t
be a VSC term.

1.

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
is diamond;
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
strongly commute.

2.

Fullness
:

t
is
\mathsf{x}
-normal if and only if
t
is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal.

IV
Multi Types by Value

We present a (linear logic based)
multi type system
for CbV, inducing the relational model for CbV.
For Plotkin’s CbV
\lambda
-calculus, it has been introduced by Ehrhard
[
25
]
, as the CbV version of de Carvalho’s System
\mathsf{R}
for CbN
[
19
,
20
]
.

The study of multi types in this section takes the shrinking technique as presented for CbN in

[
6
]
, and uses it to lift the semantical study of Open CbV by

[
30
,
8
]
to Strong CbV
3
3

3

A notable difference: in

[
30
,
8
]
there are no base types and termination is characterized using
\mathbf{0}
; here this is not possible because 1) it is incompatible with the shrinking technique, and 2) in Open CbV terminating and erasable terms coincide, while in Strong CbV they do not (see
Section

I-B
.)
.

Multi Types

There are two layers of types,
linear
and
multi types
, mutually defined by:

\begin{array}[]{r\colspace r@{\ } ll}\textsc{Linear types}&{\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{B}}&\Coloneqq{\mathsf{G}}\mid{\mathsf{M}}\multimap{\mathsf{N}}\\
\textsc{Multi types}&{\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{N}}&\Coloneqq[{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]\quad n\in\mathbb{N}\end{array}

where
{\mathsf{G}}
is an unspecified ground type and
[{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]
is our notation for finite
multisets.
The
empty
multi type
[\,]
(obtained taking
n=0
) is also denoted by
\mathbf{0}
.
A generic (multi or linear) type is denoted by
{\mathsf{T}}
.
A multi type
[{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]
has to be intended as a conjunction
{\mathsf{A}}_{1}\land\dots\land{\mathsf{A}}_{n}
of linear types
{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}
, for a commutative, associative, non-idempotent conjunction
\land
(morally a tensor
\otimes
), whose neutral element is
\mathbf{0}
.

The intuition is that a linear type corresponds to a single use of a term
t
, and that
t
is typed with a
multiset

{\mathsf{M}}
of
n
linear types if it is going to be used (at most)
n
times. The meaning of
using a term
is not
easy to define precisely. Roughly, it means that if
t
is part of a larger term
u
, then (at most)
n
copies
of
t
shall end up in evaluation position during the evaluation of
u
. More precisely, the
n
copies shall
end
up in evaluation positions where
they are applied to some terms.

\mathsf{ax}

x\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}]\vdash x\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}

@

\Gamma\vdash t\!:\![{\mathsf{M}}\!\multimap\!{\mathsf{N}}]
\Delta\vdash u\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
\Gamma\uplus\Delta\vdash tu\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}

\lambda

{\Gamma,x\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}
{\Gamma}\vdash\lambda x.{t}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}\multimap{\mathsf{N}}

\mathsf{es}

\Gamma,x\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}
\Delta\vdash u\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
\Gamma\uplus\Delta\vdash t[x{\shortleftarrow}u]\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}

\mathsf{many}

\left[{\Gamma_{i}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}_{i}\right]_{{i\in I}}
{\biguplus_{{i\in I}}\Gamma_{i}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}_{i}]_{{i\in I}}

Fig. 1
:
Call-by-Value Multi Type System. In the rule
\mathsf{many}
,
v_{\mathsf{t}}
is a
theoretical value
,
i.e.
a variable or an abstraction.

The derivation rules for the multi types system are in
Figure

1
(explanation follows). The rules are the same as in Ehrhard
[
25
]
, up to the fact that they are extended to
ES
.

A
multi
(resp.
linear
)
judgment
has the shape
\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{T}}
where
t
is a term,
{\mathsf{T}}
is a
multi (resp. linear) type and
\Gamma
is a
type context
, that is, a total function from variables to multi types
such that the set
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)\coloneqq\{x\mid\Gamma(x)\neq\mathbf{0}\}
is finite.

Technicalities about Types

The type context
\Gamma
is
empty
if
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
.

Multi-set sum

\uplus
is extended to type contexts point-wise,

i.e.

(\Gamma\uplus\Delta)(x)\coloneqq\Gamma(x)\uplus\Delta(x)
for each variable
x
.
This notion is extended to a finite family of type contexts as expected,
in particular
\biguplus_{i\in J\!}\Gamma_{i}
is the empty context when
J=\emptyset
.
A type context
\Gamma
is denoted by
x_{1}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}_{1},\dots,x_{n}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}_{n}
(for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
) if
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)\subseteq\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
and
\Gamma(x_{i})={\mathsf{M}}_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Given two type contexts
\Gamma
and
\Delta
such that
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)\cap\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)=\emptyset
, the
type
context
\Gamma,\Delta
is defined by
(\Gamma,\Delta)(x)\coloneqq\Gamma(x)
if
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
,
(\Gamma,\Delta)(x)\coloneqq\Delta(x)
if
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)
, and
(\Gamma,\Delta)(x)\coloneqq\mathbf{0}
otherwise.
Note that
\Gamma,x\!:\!\mathbf{0}=\Gamma
, where we implicitly assume
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
.

We write
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
if
\Phi
is a (
type
)
derivation
(
i.e.
a tree constructed using the rules in
Figure

1
) with conclusion the multi judgment
\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
.
In particular, we write
\Phi\vartriangleright\,\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
when
\Gamma
is empty.
We write
\Phi\vartriangleright t
if
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
for some type context
\Gamma
and some multi type
{\mathsf{M}}
.
Our study being quantitative, we use two notions of size of type derivations.

Definition IV.1

(Derivation size(s))
.

Let
\Phi
be a derivation.
The
(general) size

|\Phi|
of
\Phi
is the number of
rule occurrences in
\Phi
except for the rule
\mathsf{many}
.
The
multiplicative size

|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
of
\Phi
is the number of occurrences of the rules
\lambda
and

@
in
\Phi
.

The two sizes for a derivation play different qualitative and quantitative roles.
Qualitative
: to have a
combinatorial proof of the characterization of normalizable terms, we need a measure that decreases for some kinds of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
steps; this role is played by the general size
|\!\cdot\!|
.

Quantitative
: to count the number of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{m}}
step in some kinds of
\mathsf{vsc}
-normalizing evaluation,
i.e.
our cost model;
his role is played by the multiplicative size
|\!\cdot\!|_{\mathsf{m}}
.

Multisets and the
\mathsf{many}
rule

The rule
\mathsf{many}
plays a crucial role.
It can be applied to theoretical values only (
i.e.
variables and abstractions) and it says how many “copies” (possibly none) of a theoretical value occurring in a term
t
are needed to evaluate
t
.
It corresponds to the promotion rule of linear logic,
which, in the CbV representation of the
\lambda
-calculus, is indeed used for typing
abstractions and variables.

Some preliminary results

The two next lemmas establish a key feature of this type system: in a typed term
t
,
substituting a value for
a variable (as in the exponential step) preserves the type of
t
and “consumes” the multi type of the variable.
Besides, it also provides quantitative information about the type derivation for
t
before and after the substitution.

Lemma IV.2

(Substitution)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
D.2

Let
t
be a term,
v
be a value and
\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma,x\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
and
\Psi\vartriangleright{\Delta}\vdash v\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}
be derivations.
Then there is a derivation
\Theta\vartriangleright{\Gamma\uplus\Delta}\vdash t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v\}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}

with
|\Theta|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Theta|\leq|\Phi|+|\Psi|
.

Lemma IV.3

(Anti-substitution)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
D.4

Let
t
be a term,
v
be a value, and

\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v\}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}

be a derivation.
Then there are two derivations
\Psi\vartriangleright{\Delta,x\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}

and
\Theta\vartriangleright{\Sigma}\vdash v\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}
such that
\Gamma=\Delta\uplus\Sigma

with
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Theta|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Phi|\leq|\Psi|+|\Theta|
.

lemmas

IV.2
and
IV.3
are needed to prove subject reduction and expansion, respectively, which mean that the type can be preserved after any reduction and expansion step.

Proposition IV.4

(Qualitative subjects)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
D.5

Let
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}t^{\prime}
.

1.

Reduction
: if
\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
then there is a derivation
\Phi^{\prime}\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t^{\prime}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
such that

|\Phi^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}\leq|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Phi^{\prime}|\leq|\Phi|
.

2.

Expansion
: if
\Phi^{\prime}\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t^{\prime}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
then there is a derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
such that

|\Phi^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}\leq|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Phi^{\prime}|\leq|\Phi|
.

Proposition

IV.4
says also that the derivation sizes cannot increase (resp. decrease) after a reduction (resp. expansion) step.
However, they are not precise enough to be used for a combinatorial proof of normalization, or to extract quantitative information about the evaluation length.
There are two ways to make them precise, using always the
same
type system: we restrict either the reductions, or the kind of types taken into account.
We shall explore both directions in the next sections.

Denotational Semantics

The multi type system induces a denotational model for VSC, called
relational semantics
.
The semantics of a term is simply the set of its derivable type judgments.
More precisely,
let
t
be a term and
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
(with
n\geq 0
) be pairwise distinct variables.
If

{\sf fv}(t)\subseteq\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, the list
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is
suitable
for
t
.
If

\vec{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is suitable for
t
, the (
relational
)
semantics
of
t
for
\vec{x}
is:

\displaystyle\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\vec{x}}=\{(({\mathsf{N}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{N}}_{n}),{\mathsf{M}})\mid\exists\,\Phi\vartriangleright x_{1}\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}_{1},\dots,x_{n}\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}_{n}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}\}.

Subject reduction and expansion (
Proposition

IV.4
) guarantee that the semantics of a term is invariant under evaluation,
i.e.
relational semantics is a denotational model for VSC.

Proposition IV.5

(Invariance under evaluation)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
D.6

Let
\vec{x}
be suitable for
t
.
If
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}t^{\prime}
then
\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\vec{x}}=\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\vec{x}}
.

A Uniform Approach

In
Sections

V
and
VI
, we use the
same
given multi type system to characterize normalization of two different evaluation strategies in VSC, namely open
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
(defined in
Section

II
) and external
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
(defined in
Section

III
), verifying
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}\,\subsetneq\,\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
. In both case proving that, given an evaluation
\to_{\alpha}
, a term is
\alpha
-normalizing if and only if is typable, and extracting bounds on the evaluation length from type derivations (kind 1).

The two cases follow the same pattern, whose blueprint is given by the simpler open case.
Each case shall use some predicates (inert, tight, unitary, shrinking) to isolate the derivations characterizing termination or providing the right bounds for that case, often needing more than one predicate, for different things.
The predicates shall work by restricting the types appearing in the final judgment of the derivation—this way isolating fragments of the denotational semantics.
Sometimes, often for the induction to go through, the predicate concerns only the typing context, or only the right-hand term, and not the whole final judgment.

The Special Role of Inert Terms

Another recurrent ingredient are (
strong
) inert terms. In statements about normal forms, they usually satisfy stronger properties, essential for the induction to go through. Predicates shall also
spread
on inert terms: if assumed on the type context, they transfer to the right-hand type, which is in turn the key step to propagate the predicate on sub-derivations.
For bounds of kind 1, we give the full statements. For bounds of kind 2 and 3, more technical, the full statements are only in the Appendix, for readability.

V
Multi Types for Open CbV

Here we recall the relationship between CbV multi types and Open CbV developed by Accattoli and Guerrieri in

[
8
]
. The reason is threefold. First, the external case relies on the open one.
Second, it provides the blueprint for the development of the strong case, allowing us to stress similarities and differences.
Last, the development in
[
8
]
needs to be slightly adapted to our present framework.
Namely, here we use the Open VSC instead of the split fireball calculus used in
[
8
]
(another formalism for Open CbV),
and we include a ground type
{\mathsf{G}}
, necessary to later deal with the external strategy (for Open CbV the empty multi type
\mathbf{0}
would suffice).

Qualitatively, the open evaluation of
t
terminates if and only if
t
is typable.
Since
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
does not reduce under abstractions, every abstraction is
{\mathsf{o}}
-normal and hence must be typable: for this reason,
\lambda x.\delta\delta
is typable with
\mathbf{0}
(take the derivation only made of one rule
\mathsf{many}
with
0
premises), though
\delta\delta
is not.

Quantitatively, the multiplicative size

|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
of the derivation
\Phi
bounds the sum of the length of the open evaluation plus
the open size of its open normal form. The bound is exact when
\Phi
satisfies the
tight
predicate, defined below.

Inert and Tight Derivations

A multi type is
ground
if it is
n[{\mathsf{G}}]\coloneqq[{\mathsf{G}},\dots,{\mathsf{G}}]
(
n
times
{\mathsf{G}}
) for any
n\geq 0
(so,
0[{\mathsf{G}}]=\mathbf{0}
).

\begin{array}[]{r r@{\ } ll}\textsc{Inert multi type}&{\mathsf{M}}^{\mathsf{i}}&\Coloneqq[{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathsf{i}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathsf{i}}_{n}]&n\geq 0\\
\textsc{Inert linear type}&{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathsf{i}}&\Coloneqq{\mathsf{G}}\mid n[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap{\mathsf{M}}^{\mathsf{i}}&n\geq 0\end{array}

A type context
x_{1}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}_{1},\dots,x_{n}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}_{n}
is
inert
if
{\mathsf{M}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{M}}_{n}
are inert multi types.
Note that every ground multi type is inert.

A derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
is
inert
if
\Gamma
is an inert type context and
{\mathsf{M}}
is an inert multi type. If, moreover,
{\mathsf{M}}
is a ground multi type, then
\Phi
is
tight
.
Note that the definitions of inert and tight derivation depend only on its final judgment.

Tight
derivations are those we are actually interested in, but often, for the induction to go through, we have to consider the wider class of inert derivations.
For instance, tight derivations may have a complex
structure, having sub-derivations for inert terms that might
not be tight, but only inert.
Also, inert types spread on inert terms—the first key property of inert terms.

Lemma V.1

(Spreading of inertness on judgments)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
E.1

Let
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash i\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
be a derivation and
i
be an inert term.
If
\Gamma
is a inert type context, then
{\mathsf{M}}
is a inert multi type (and so
\Phi
is inert).

Correctness

Open correctness establishes that all typable terms
{\mathsf{o}}
-normalize and the multiplicative size of the derivation bounds the number of
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
steps plus the open size of the
{\mathsf{o}}
-normal form; this bound is exact if the derivation is tight.
Open correctness is proved following a standard scheme in two stages:
(a)

quantitative
subject reduction states that every
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
step preserves types and decreases the general size of a derivation, and that any
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
step decreases by an exact quantity the multiplicative size of a derivation;

(b)
a lemma states that the multiplicative size of any derivation typing a
{\mathsf{o}}
-normal form
t
provides an
upper bound
to the open size of
t
, and if moreover the derivation is tight then the bound is
exact
. The lemma follows—its unusual statement puts forward

Lemma V.2

(Size of fireballs)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
E.2

Let
f
be a fireball.
If
\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash f\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}

then
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}\geq|f|_{{\mathsf{o}}}
.
If, moreover,
\Gamma
is inert and (
{\mathsf{M}}
is ground inert or
f
is inert), then

|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=|f|_{{\mathsf{o}}}
.

Example
. The non-inert fireball
\delta\coloneqq\lambda x.{xx}
is typable and the last rule of any derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash\delta\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
is
\mathsf{many}
.
If
{\mathsf{M}}
is not ground then
\mathsf{many}
has at least one premise that types the subterm
xx
, so
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}>|\delta|_{{\mathsf{o}}}
.
If
{\mathsf{M}}
is ground, then
{\mathsf{M}}=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathsf{many}
has no premises, with
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=0=|\delta|_{{\mathsf{o}}}
.
For inert terms,
Lemma

V.2
says that their open size is the multiplicative size of their
inert
derivations;
by spreading of inertness (
Lemma

V.1
), it amounts to say that the type context is inert.

Proposition V.3

(Open quantitative subject reduction)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
E.3

Let
\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
be a derivation.

1.

Multiplicative step:
if
t\rightarrow_{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}
then there is a derivation

\Phi^{\prime}\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t^{\prime}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
with

|\Phi^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}-2
and
|\Phi^{\prime}|=|\Phi|-1
;

2.

Exponential step:
if
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}t^{\prime}
then there is a derivation

\Phi^{\prime}\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t^{\prime}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
such that

|\Phi^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Phi^{\prime}|<|\Phi|
.

As the general size of derivations decreases after any
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
step,
we have a combinatorial proof of open correctness.

Theorem V.4

(Open correctness)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
E.4

Let
\Phi\vartriangleright t
be a derivation.
Then there is a
{\mathsf{o}}
-normalizing evaluation
d\colon t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}^{*}u
with
2|d|_{\mathsf{m}}+|u|_{{\mathsf{o}}}\leq|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
.
And if
\Phi
is tight, then
2|d|_{\mathsf{m}}+|u|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
.

Completeness

Open completeness establishes that every
{\mathsf{o}}
-normalizing term is typable, and with a tight derivation
\Phi
such that
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
is exactly the number of
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
steps plus the open size of the
{\mathsf{o}}
-normal form.
Again, the proof technique is standard:
(a)

quantitative
subject expansion states that typability can be pulled back along
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
steps, increasing
|\Phi|
;

(b)
a lemma states that every
{\mathsf{o}}
-normal form is typable with a
tight
derivation—inert terms verify a stronger statement.

Lemma V.5

(Tight typability of open normal forms)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
E.5

1.

Inert:
if
t
is an inert term then, for any inert multi
type
{\mathsf{M}}
, there is an inert derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
.

2.

Fireball:
if
t
is a fireball then there is a tight derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!\mathbf{0}
.

Proposition V.6

(Open quantitative subject expansion)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
E.6

Let
\Phi^{\prime}\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t^{\prime}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
be a derivation.

1.

Multiplicative step:
if
t\rightarrow_{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}
then there is a derivation

\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
with

|\Phi^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}-2
and
|\Phi^{\prime}|=|\Phi|-1
;

2.

Exponential step:
if
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}t^{\prime}
then there is a derivation

\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
such that

|\Phi^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Phi^{\prime}|<|\Phi|
.

As the general size of derivations increases after a backward
{\mathsf{o}}
-step,
a combinatorial proof of open completeness follows.

Theorem V.7

(Open completeness)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
E.7

Let
d\colon t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}^{*}u
be an
{\mathsf{o}}
-normalizing evaluation.
Then there is a tight derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!\mathbf{0}
such that
2|d|_{\mathsf{m}}+|f|_{{\mathsf{s}}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
.

Adequate denotational semantics for
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}

Relational semantics is a denotational model for
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
(
Proposition

IV.5
).
Moreover, it is
adequate
for
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
: it provides a semantic characterization of all and only the terminating terms for
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
, thanks to correctness (
Theorem

VI.4
) and completeness (
Theorem

V.7
).

Theorem V.8

(Open adequacy)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
E.8

Let
t
be a term, with
\vec{x}
suitable for
t
.
Then,
t
is
{\mathsf{o}}
-normalizing if and only if
\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\vec{x}}\neq\emptyset
.

VI
Shrinking Multi Types for the External Strategy

\begin{array}[]{rrcl \colspace rrcl}\text{Right multi type }&{\mathsf{M}}^{{\textsc{r}}}&\Coloneqq&[{\mathsf{A}}^{{\textsc{r}}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}^{{\textsc{r}}}_{n}]\ \ n\geq 1&\text{Right linear type }&{\mathsf{A}}^{{\textsc{r}}}&\Coloneqq&{\mathsf{G}}\mid{\mathsf{M}}^{\textsc{l}}\multimap{\mathsf{A}}^{{\textsc{r}}}\\
\text{Left multi type }&{\mathsf{M}}^{\textsc{l}}&\Coloneqq&[{\mathsf{A}}^{\textsc{l}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}^{\textsc{l}}_{n}]\ \ n\geq 0&\text{Left linear type }&{\mathsf{A}}^{\textsc{l}}&\Coloneqq&{\mathsf{G}}\mid{\mathsf{M}}^{{\textsc{r}}}\multimap{\mathsf{M}}^{\textsc{l}}\\[5.0pt]
\text{Unitary right multi type }&{\mathsf{M}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{r}}}&\Coloneqq&[{\mathsf{A}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{r}}}]&\text{Unitary right linear type }&{\mathsf{A}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{r}}}&\Coloneqq&{\mathsf{G}}\mid{\mathsf{M}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{l}}}\multimap{\mathsf{M}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{r}}}\\
\text{Unitary left multi type }&{\mathsf{M}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{l}}}&\Coloneqq&[{\mathsf{A}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{l}}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{l}}}_{n}]\ \ n\geq 0&\text{Unitary left linear type }&{\mathsf{A}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{l}}}&\Coloneqq&{\mathsf{G}}\mid{\mathsf{M}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{r}}}\multimap{\mathsf{M}}^{{\textsc{u}}{\textsc{l}}}\end{array}

Fig. 2
:
Kinds of right and left (shrinking) types.

In Strong CbV, diverging terms may be typable.
For instance,
x(\lambda y.\delta\delta)
(with
\delta\coloneqq\lambda z.zz
) can be typed by assigning
[\mathbf{0}\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]]
to
x
, and typing the diverging argument
(\lambda y.\delta\delta)
with
\mathbf{0}
(via a
\mathsf{many}
rule with 0 premises).
The point is that, since
\mathbf{0}
is on the left of
\multimap
, the argument is meant to be erased, but
x
cannot actually
erase it. This is a problem typical of strong settings, as it occurs also in Strong CbN. The solution is to restrict
to type derivations satisfying a predicate that forbids types where
\mathbf{0}
plays these dangerous tricks. This is
unavoidable and standard, see
[
36
,
6
]
. Following
[
6
]
, the predicate is here called
shrinkingness

because it ensures that the size of type derivations shrinks at each
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
step (see
Propositions

VI.3
and
VI.6
).

Since the typing rule
\lambda
shifts a multi type from the left-hand side of a judgment to the left of
\multimap
on the right-hand side of a judgment, we actually need two notions of shrinking types,
left
and
right
.
Indeed, a judgment is shrinking (and so it ensures that the size of the derivation shrinks at each
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
step) when the typing context is made of left shrinking multi types, and the type on the right-hand side is right shrinking.
Formally,
left
and
right shrinking
(multi or linear)
types
are defined in
Figure

2
(we often omit “shrinking” when referring to left or right types, for brevity), by mutual induction: they are somehow dual notions.
Additionally, we need
unitary (left/right) shrinkingness
(see
Figure

2
) to
characterize those minimal derivations that measure

exactly
the number of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
steps to normal form and the size of the normal form:
indeed,
\mathsf{x}
-evaluation reduces under abstractions and unitality requires to take just one “copy” of the body of the abstraction.
The notions extend to type contexts and to derivations as follows:

•

A type context
x_{1}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}_{1},\dots,x_{n}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}_{n}
is
left

(resp.
unitary
left

) if each
{\mathsf{M}}_{i}
is
left
(resp. unitary
left
).

•

A derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
is
shrinking
(resp.
unitary shrinking
) if
\Gamma
is
left
(resp. unitary
left
) and
{\mathsf{M}}
is
right

(resp. unitary
right
).

Of course, (right/left) unitary shrinkingness implies shrinkingness, but the converse is false—just consider
[{\mathsf{G}},{\mathsf{G}}]
.
Note that
[{\mathsf{G}}]
is both unitary
left
and unitary
right
, while
\mathbf{0}
is unitary
left
but not
right
, and
[\mathbf{0}\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]]
is unitary
right
but not
left
.

The important notion is the one of (unitary) shrinking derivation, but often, for the induction to go through, statements have a less intuitive form, typically concerning inertness or type contexts alone.
Shrinkingness is a predicate of derivations defined by looking only at the final judgment. For proving
properties of shrinking derivations, we have to analyze how shrinking propagates to sub-derivations. The following lemma is specific to
left

shrinkingness, on which the propagation of shrinkingness then build.
It says that for a specific kind of terms—typable
rigid
terms—
left
shrinkingess spreads from the type context to the right-hand multi type in a judgment.

Lemma VI.1

(Spreading of
left
shrinkingness on judgments)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
F.1

Let
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash r\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
be a derivation and
r
a rigid term.
If
\Gamma
is a
left
(
resp.
unitary
left
) type context, then
{\mathsf{M}}
is a
left
(
resp.
unitary
left
) multi type.

To prove correctness (
Theorem

VI.4
) and completeness (
Theorem

VI.7
) of shrinking derivation with respect to the external strategy, we follow the same pattern as for the open case.

Correctness

Shrinking correctness establishes that all typable terms with a shrinking derivation
\mathsf{x}
-normalize and the multiplicative size of the derivation bounds the number of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}\mathsf{m}}
steps plus the
strong
size of the
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal form; this bound is exact if the derivation is unitary shrinking.

Lemma VI.2

(Size of
strong
fireballs)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
F.2

Let
\Gamma
be a
left
(
resp.
unitary
left
) type context,
f_{\mathsf{s}}
be a
strong
fireball and
\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash f_{\mathsf{s}}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
be a derivation such that if
f_{\mathsf{s}}
is an
answer
then
{\mathsf{M}}
is
right
(
resp.
unitary
right
).
Then
|f_{\mathsf{s}}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}\leq|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
(resp.
|f_{\mathsf{s}}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
).

Note that
Lemma

VI.2
applies in particular to any shrinking and unitary shrinking derivation of a
strong
fireball.
The more involved statement is required because when
f_{\mathsf{s}}=i_{\mathsf{s}}f^{\prime}_{\mathsf{s}}
(which is not an
answer
) its multi type
{\mathsf{M}}
need not be
right
. Indeed, the subterm
i_{\mathsf{s}}
(which is inert and so rigid) has type
[{\mathsf{N}}\multimap{\mathsf{M}}]
and the spreading of
left
shrinkingness (
Lemma

VI.1
) ensures that
[{\mathsf{N}}\multimap{\mathsf{M}}]
is
left
, but it does not imply that
{\mathsf{M}}
is
right
.

Proposition VI.3

(Shrinking quantitative subject reduction)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
F.3

Let
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
with
\Gamma

left
(
resp.
unitary
left
).
Assume that if
t
is an
answer
, then
{\mathsf{M}}
is
right
(
resp.
unitary
right
).

1.

Multiplicative step:
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}\mathsf{m}}t^{\prime}
then there is a derivation
\Phi^{\prime}\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
such
that

|\Phi^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}\leq|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}-2
and
|\Phi^{\prime}|<|\Phi|

(
resp.

|\Phi^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}-2
and
|\Phi^{\prime}|=|\Phi|-1
).

2.

Exponential step:
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{e}}}t^{\prime}
then there is a derivation
\Phi^{\prime}\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
such that

|\Phi^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Phi^{\prime}|<|\Phi|
.

Theorem VI.4

(Shrinking correctness)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
F.4

Let
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
be a shrinking (
resp.
unitary shrinking) derivation.
Then there are a
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal form
t^{\prime}
and an evaluation
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}^{*}t^{\prime}
with
2|d|_{\mathsf{m}}+|t^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}\leq|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
(
resp.

2|d|_{\mathsf{m}}+|t^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
).

Completeness.

Shrinking completeness establishes that every
\mathsf{x}
-normalizing term is typable with a unitary shrinking derivation whose multiplicative size is exactly the number of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
steps plus the
strong
size of the
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal form.

Lemma VI.5

(Shrinking typability of normal forms)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
F.5

1.

Inert:

for any
strong
inert term
i_{\mathsf{s}}
and
left
multi type
{\mathsf{M}}
,
there is a
left
type context
\Gamma

and a derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash i_{\mathsf{s}}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
.
If moreover
{\mathsf{M}}
is unitary, then so is
\Gamma
.

2.

Fireball:

for every
strong
fireball
f_{\mathsf{s}}

there exists a unitary shrinking derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash f_{\mathsf{s}}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
.

The proof of
Lemma

VI.5
relies on the ground type
{\mathsf{G}}
, which is both unitary
left
and unitary
right
.
In this way, Point
1
(needed to have a stronger
i.h.
) can be used to prove Point
2
.

(Shrinking quantitative subject expansion)
.
†

margin:

Let

.
Assume that if
t^{\prime}
is an

{\mathsf{M}}
is unitary

.

1.

Multiplicative step:
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}\mathsf{m}}t^{\prime}
then there is a derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
such that

|\Phi^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}\leq|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}-2
and
|\Phi^{\prime}|<|\Phi|

(

and

);

if
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{e}}}t^{\prime}

(Shrinking completeness)
.

\mathsf{vsc}

.

VII
Normalization and Adequacy

In our multi type system, subject reduction and expansion hold for the whole reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
, without quantitative information (
Proposition

IV.4
).
Combining this with shrinking correctness and completeness—which hold only for the external strategy—we obtain two notable results: 1) a
normalization
theorem for VSC, by exploiting an elegant proof technique used in
[
21
,
40
]
; 2) we provide an
adequate
model for the whole VSC.

Theorem VII.1

(Operational and semantic characterizations of normalization)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
G.1

Let
t
be a term. The following are equivalent:

1.

there is a
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal form
u
such that
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}u
;

2.

t
is
\mathsf{x}
-normalizing;

3.

\llbracket t\rrbracket^{\mathsf{s}}_{\vec{x}}\neq\emptyset
, where
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is suitable for
t
and

\begin{split}\llbracket t\rrbracket^{\mathsf{s}}_{\vec{x}}\coloneqq\{(({\mathsf{N}}_{1}&,\dots,{\mathsf{N}}_{n}),{\mathsf{M}})\mid\exists\,\Phi\vartriangleright x_{1}\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}_{1},\dots,x_{n}\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}_{n}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}\mbox{ such that ${\mathsf{N}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{N}}_{n}$ are \text{left} and ${\mathsf{M}}$ is \text{right} }\}.\end{split}

This is our main qualitative result for
Strong
CbV.
Quantitatively, we provided bounds from type derivation (kind 1).

@

\mathsf{many}

\lambda

\mathsf{many}

\mathsf{ax}

\lambda

\mathsf{many}

\mathsf{ax}

\mathsf{many}

\lambda

@

\mathsf{many}

\mathsf{ax}

\mathsf{many}

\mathsf{ax}

x\!:\![[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]]\vdash x\!:\![{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]

x\!:\![[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]]\vdash x\!:\![[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]]

:

\Psi_{\delta{\mathsf{I}}}
