.

VIII
Bounds of Kind 2 and 3, Revisited

Up to now, we studied bounds of kind 1 (bounds from type derivations), now we turn to bounds of kind 2 and 3 for the external strategy.
We start with kind 2 (bounds from types), use them to understand kind 3 (bounds from composable types), and then use kind 3 to obtain exact bounds of kind 2.

Types Size and Bounds from Types

With multi types, not only type derivations but also multi types provide quantitative information, in this case on the size of normal forms.
To see it, we have to define a size for types and type contexts, which is simply the number of occurrences of
\multimap
.
Formally, the
size
of linear and multi types is defined by mutual induction by:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}l@{\,} l@{\quad} r@{\,}l@{\,} l@{\quad} c@{\,}c@{\,}c}|{\mathsf{G}}|&\coloneqq&0&|{\mathsf{M}}\multimap{\mathsf{N}}|&\coloneqq&1\!+\!|{\mathsf{M}}|\!+\!|{\mathsf{N}}|&|[{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]|&\coloneqq&{\textstyle{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}}|{\mathsf{A}}_{i}|\end{array}

Clearly,
|{\mathsf{T}}|\geq 0
and
|{\mathsf{M}}|=0
if and only if
{\mathsf{M}}=n[{\mathsf{G}}]
(
n\geq 0)
.

Given a type context
\Gamma=x_{1}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}_{1},\dots,x_{n}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}_{n}
we often consider the list of
its types, noted
\hat{\Gamma}\coloneqq({\mathsf{M}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{M}}_{n})
. Since any list of multi types
({\mathsf{M}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{M}}_{n})
can be seen as extracted from a type context
\Gamma
, we use the notation
\hat{\Gamma}
for lists of multi types.
The
size
of a list of multi types is
|({\mathsf{M}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{M}}_{n})|\allowbreak\coloneqq\allowbreak\sum_{i=1}^{n}|{\mathsf{M}}_{i}|
, and of the conclusion
of a derivation
\pi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash e\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
is
|(\hat{\Gamma},{\mathsf{M}})|\coloneqq|\hat{\Gamma}|+|{\mathsf{M}}|
.
Clearly,
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
implies
|\hat{\Gamma}|=0
.

Bounds of Kind 2

Types bound the size of normal forms—this is kind 2. It is however instructive to decompose this fact, by showing that more generally, types bound the size of type derivations for normal terms (which in turn bound the size of normal forms, as we already know).

Proposition VIII.1

(Types bound the size of derivations for normal forms)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
H.1

Let
t
be a
strong
fireball and
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
be a derivation. Then

|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}\leq|\hat{\Gamma}|+|{\mathsf{M}}|
.

Note that we are not restricting to shrinking derivations.

Perhaps surprisingly, the bound may not be exact, as a forthcoming example shows—that is, there can be a gap. As expected, we shall see that minimal types give exact bounds.

Composing with the bound from shrinking derivations (Lemma
VI.2
), we obtain bounds of kind 2.

Corollary VIII.2

(Types bound the size of
strong
fireballs)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
H.2

Let
t
be a
strong
fireball and
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
be a shrinking derivation. Then
|t|_{{\mathsf{s}}}\leq|{\mathsf{M}}|+|\hat{\Gamma}|
.

Lax Bounds of Kind 3

De Carvalho’s idea is that, given two normal forms
t
and
u
, one can extract bounds for the evaluation of
tu
by truly
semantic means
, by looking only at the types of
t
and
u
—that is, at
\llbracket t\rrbracket
and
\llbracket u\rrbracket
—because a derivation for
tu
is just the application of a derivation for
t
and one for
u
.
Remark
: in formulating bounds of kind 3 we restrict to closed terms for the sake of simplicity (it allows to ignore the type context)—de Carvalho does the same. There are however no issues in dealing with open terms.

Definition VIII.3

(Composable pairs)
.

Let
t
and
u
be closed normal terms. The set of composable pairs of
t
and
u
is:

U(t,u)\coloneqq\{({\mathsf{M}}\multimap{\mathsf{N}},{\mathsf{M}})\ |\ {\mathsf{M}}\multimap{\mathsf{N}}\in\llbracket t\rrbracket\mbox{ and }{\mathsf{M}}\in\llbracket u\rrbracket\mbox{ s.t. }{\mathsf{N}}\mbox{ is right}\}
.

Theorem VIII.4

(Lax bounds of kind 3)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
H.3

Let
t
and
u
be closed normalizing terms. Then
tu
normalizes if and only if
U(t,u)\neq\emptyset
. Moreover, if
t
and
u
are normal,
d:tu\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}^{*}s
, and
s
is normal then
2|d|_{\mathsf{m}}+|s|_{{\mathsf{s}}}\leq|{\mathsf{M}}|+|{\mathsf{N}}|+1
for every composable pair
({\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{N}})\in U(t,u)
.

Now, the tricky point is how to obtain
exact
bounds. For a term in isolation, exact bounds of kind 2 are given by minimal types, as we shall prove. The problem is that, instead, for the application of two terms (kind 3) the minimal composable pair does not provide exact bounds. This happens because minimal types do not compose—said differently, the minimal composable pair is not made out of two minimal types for
t
and
u
. The following example pinpoints the subtleties.

Key Example

Consider the unitary shrinking derivations
\Phi_{\delta}
and
\Phi_{\mathsf{I}}
of minimal types for
\delta=\lambda x.xx
and for
{\mathsf{I}}=\lambda y.y
:

\Phi_{\delta}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\>\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{many}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 10.10759pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\lambda$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 22.25197pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$@$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 6.47491pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\>\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{many}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 21.51375pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{ax}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\>\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{many}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 53.37465pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{ax}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{$x\!:\![[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]]\vdash x\!:\![{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$x\!:\![[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]]\vdash x\!:\![[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]]$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{$x\!:\![{\mathsf{G}}]\vdash x\!:\!{\mathsf{G}}$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$x\!:\![{\mathsf{G}}]\vdash x\!:\![{\mathsf{G}}]$}}\par}\par}}}}\hbox{$x\!:\![[[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}],{\mathsf{G}}]]\vdash xx\!:\![{\mathsf{G}}]$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$\vdash\lambda x.xx\!:\![[[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}],{\mathsf{G}}]]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$\vdash\lambda x.xx\!:\![[[[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}],{\mathsf{G}}]]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]]$}}\par}\par}}}$}

and

\Phi_{\mathsf{I}}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\>\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{many}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 10.10759pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\lambda$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\>\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{many}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 21.14912pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{ax}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{$y\!:\![{\mathsf{G}}]\vdash y\!:\!{\mathsf{G}}$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$y\!:\![{\mathsf{G}}]\vdash y\!:\![{\mathsf{G}}]$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$\vdash\lambda y.y\!:\![{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$\vdash\lambda y.y\!:\![[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]]$}}\par}\par}}}$}

Note that, pleasantly,
|\Phi_{\delta}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\delta|_{{\mathsf{s}}}=|[[[[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}],{\mathsf{G}}]]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]]|=2
, and
|\Phi_{\mathsf{I}}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|{\mathsf{I}}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}=|[[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]]|=1
.
Let’s now consider the application
\delta{\mathsf{I}}
. Unfortunately, the two obtained minimal types do not compose. Now, consider the unitary shrinking derivation
\Psi_{\delta{\mathsf{I}}}
for
\delta{\mathsf{I}}
with minimal types (which provides exact information for
\delta{\mathsf{I}}
) in Fig.
3
. Note that its sub-derivations for
\Psi_{\delta}
and
\Psi_{\mathsf{I}}
for
\delta
and
{\mathsf{I}}
do not derive minimal types. The derivation
\Psi_{\delta{\mathsf{I}}}
indeed is obtained by composing

1.

the variant
\Psi_{\delta}
of
\Phi_{\delta}
which has the same exact structure of
\Phi_{\delta}
and where every occurrence of
{\mathsf{G}}
has been replaced with
{\mathsf{G}}^{2}\coloneqq[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]
, obtaining the type
[[[[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}],{{\mathsf{G}}^{2}}]]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]]
.

2.

with two derivations for
{\mathsf{I}}
, one being
\Phi_{\mathsf{I}}
(which has type
[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]
), and one being the variant
\Phi_{\mathsf{I}}^{\prime}
of
\Phi_{\mathsf{I}}
where
{\mathsf{G}}
has been replaced with
[{\mathsf{G}}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}]
(of type
[[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]]
).

Note that there is a mismatch: since the types derived by
\Psi_{\delta}
and
\Psi_{\mathsf{I}}
are not minimal, their sizes are bigger than
|\Psi_{\delta}|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Psi_{\mathsf{I}}|_{\mathsf{m}}
, and so they do not provide exact bounds for
\delta{\mathsf{I}}
. Namely, the size of
[[[[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}],{{\mathsf{G}}^{2}}]]\multimap[{\mathsf{G}}^{2}]]
, which is the type of
\Psi_{\delta}
, is 6, while
|\Psi_{\delta}|_{\mathsf{m}}=2
—this is an instance of the mentioned gap.
Summing up,
minimal types do not compose
, and
composable types do not give exact bounds
.

Out of the Impasse

De Carvalho solves this apparent
cul-de-sac
via a technical study relating composable pairs of types, on one side, and minimal types for the derivations of composable pairs, on the other side, via type substitutions. An especially puzzling point is that his approach requires the type system to have an infinity of ground types, while the quantitative properties of multi types never do.

The
composable vs minimal
tension stems from a fact about derivations in isolation, namely the mentioned
gap
: given a derivation
\Psi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
for a normal term
t
, in general
|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}
is bound by
|(\hat{\Gamma},{\mathsf{M}})|
, not equal to it.
The key point behind de Carvalho’s solution is that there always exists a re-typing
\Phi
of
\Psi
(more precisely, a derivation with the same structure and different types) whose types have the same size of
\Psi
(and
\Phi
)—the derivation
\Phi
is obtained from
\Psi
by minimizing the types introduced by axioms—in the example, this is the case for
\Psi_{\delta}
and
\Phi_{\delta}
. We deem this fact the
size representation property
. It holds independently of the number of ground types, and we claim it being the key property at work in semantical bounds—its isolation is a contribution of this work.

Skeletons

For the property, we need to formalize the notion of derivations having the same structure. The equivalence
\sim

relates derivations having the same rules arranged in the same way, but not necessarily having the same types.

Definition VIII.5

(Skeleton equivalence)
.

Let
t
be a term.
Two derivations
\Phi\vartriangleright t
and
\Psi\vartriangleright t
are
skeleton equivalent
, noted
\Phi\sim\Psi
, if they end with the same kind of rule and the derivations on the
premises are
\sim
-equivalent, namely:

•

Both
\Phi
and
\Psi
are axioms.

•

Both
\Phi
and
\Psi
end with rule
@
, their two left premises
\Phi_{l}
and
\Psi_{l}

satisfy
\Phi_{l}\sim\Psi_{l}
, and their right premises
\Phi_{r}
and
\Psi_{r}
satisfy
\Phi_{r}\sim\Psi_{r}
—similarly for rules
\lambda
and
\mathsf{es}
.

•

Both
\Phi
and
\Psi
end with a rule
\mathsf{many}
with
n
premises and there is a permutation

\sigma
of
\{1,\dots,n\}
such that the
i
-th premise
\Phi_{i}
of
\Phi
and the
\sigma(i)
-th premise

\Psi_{\sigma(i)}
of
\Psi
satisfy
\Phi_{i}\sim\Psi_{\sigma(i)}
for

i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

The next lemma shows that skeleton equivalence preserves more or less everything you can imagine, but types. It is used to prove the size representation property (
Proposition

VIII.7
).
We denote by
\#m
the cardinality of a multiset
m
.

Lemma VIII.6

(
\sim
-Invariants)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
H.4

Let
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
and
\Psi\vartriangleright\Delta\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}
be two derivations such
that
\Phi\sim\Psi
. Then
|\Phi|=|\Psi|
,
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}
,

\#{\mathsf{M}}=\#{\mathsf{N}}
,
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)=\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)
, and
\#(\Gamma(x))=\#(\Delta(x))

for every variable
x
. Moreover,
\Phi
is shrinking (resp. unitary shrinking) if and only if
\Psi
is.

Proposition VIII.7

(Size representation)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
H.5

Let
t
be a
strong
fireball and
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
be a derivation. Then there is a derivation
\Psi\vartriangleright\Delta\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}
such that
\Psi\sim\Phi
and

|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\hat{\Delta}|+|{\mathsf{N}}|
.

Note that, since in general types bound the size of the derivation (
Proposition

VIII.1
), the size representation property is stating that the types of
\Psi
are minimal.

Weak Exact Bounds of Kind 3

The size representation property induces bounds of kind 3, but only of a weak form.

Theorem VIII.8

(Weak exact bounds of kind 3)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
H.6

Let
t
and
u
be normal.
If
d:tu\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}^{*}s
and
s
is normal then there
exist
{\mathsf{M}}\in\llbracket t\rrbracket
and
{\mathsf{N}}\in\llbracket u\rrbracket
such that
2|d|_{\mathsf{m}}+|s|_{{\mathsf{s}}}=|{\mathsf{M}}|+|{\mathsf{N}}|+1
.

We obtained exact bounds, but we have lost the fact that the involved types form a composable pair, because in general the types providing the bound do not compose, as the example about
\delta{\mathsf{I}}
shows. This is the weakness of
Theorem

VIII.8
.

We can now prove de Carvalho’s original statement, connecting exactness with composability. The refinement however amounts to quite mechanical technicalities, which is why we claim that the essence is in the size representation property.

Dissecting Size Representations

We now provide a refinement of size representation. The refinement
requires the variant of the type system where the grammar of types has an infinity of ground types

\{{\mathsf{G}}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
(instead of having just
{\mathsf{G}}
), whose judgments are noted
\vdash^{\infty}
. The
improvement is that given
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash^{\infty}t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
we can find a derivation

\Psi\vartriangleright\Delta\vdash^{\infty}t\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}
and a substitution
\sigma
such that
\sigma({\mathsf{N}})={\mathsf{M}}

and
\sigma(\Delta)=\Gamma
—we call the pair
(\sigma,\Psi)
a
dissection
.

Technicalities about Substitutions

A substitution
\sigma
is a function from ground types to
linear types that is the identity but a for finite number of ground types. It is extended to act on types, multi types,
type contexts, and derivations as expected. We use
{\sf gt}(\Phi)
for
the set of ground types occurring in
\Phi
.

Definition VIII.9

(Dissections)
.

Let
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash^{\infty}t\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}
be a derivation. A
dissection
of
\Phi
is a pair
(\Psi,\sigma)
where
\Psi\vartriangleright\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash^{\infty}t\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}^{\prime}
is a derivation and
\sigma
is a substitution such that:

1.

Skeletons
:
\Psi\sim\Phi
;

2.

Representation
:
\sigma(\Psi)=\Phi
;

3.

Disjoint names
:
{\sf gt}(\Psi)\cap{\sf gt}(\Phi)=\emptyset
.

And if
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\hat{\Gamma^{\prime}}|+|{\mathsf{N}}^{\prime}|
then
(\Psi,\sigma)
is a
size
dissection
of
\Phi
.

Any derivation for a
strong
fireball admits a size dissection.

Lemma VIII.10

(Size dissection)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
H.7

Let
t
be a
strong
fireball. For any
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash^{\infty}t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}

there are a
substitution
\sigma
and a derivation
\Psi\vartriangleright\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash^{\infty}t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
forming a size dissection of

\Phi
.

Finally, we use size dissections to retrieve the CbV analogous de Carvalho’s result, stating that the types providing the bound are the smallest ones which can be turned into a composable pair via a type substitution.

Theorem VIII.11

(Exact bounds of kind 3)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
H.8

Let
t
and
u
be closed
strong
fireballs. If
d\colon tu\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}^{*}s
and
s
is normal
then
2|d|_{\mathsf{m}}+|s|_{{\mathsf{s}}}=\inf\{|{\mathsf{N}}\multimap{\mathsf{M}}|+|{\mathsf{O}}|+1\ |\ \exists\sigma\mbox{ such that }(\sigma({\mathsf{N}})\multimap\sigma({\mathsf{M}}),\sigma({\mathsf{O}}))\in U(t,u)\}
.

Exact Bounds of Kind 2

As for kind 2, we obtain exact bounds as a corollary of size representation (
Proposition

VIII.7
), by composing it with the exact bounds from derivations. Here, exactness comes from plain minimality, as usual.

Corollary VIII.12

(Minimal types catch the size of normal forms)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
H.9

Let
t
be a
strong
fireball. There exists a unitary shrinking derivation
\Phi\vartriangleright\Gamma\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
such
that
|t|_{{\mathsf{s}}}=|{\mathsf{M}}|+|\hat{\Gamma}|
.

Appendix A
Preliminaries and Notations in Rewriting

For a relation
R
on a set of terms,
R^{*}
is its reflexive-transitive closure.
Given a relation
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
, an
\mathsf{r}
-
evaluation

(or simply evaluation if unambiguous)
d
is a finite sequence of terms
(t_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n}
(for some
n\geq 0
) such that
t_{i}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}t_{i+1}
for all
1\leq i<n
, and we write
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}u
if
t_{0}=t
and
t_{n}=u
. The

length

n
of
d
is denoted by
|d|
, and
|d|_{a}
is the number of
a
-
steps
(
i.e.
the number of
t_{i}\rightarrow_{a}t_{i+1}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
) in
d
, for a given subrelation
\rightarrow_{a}
of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
.

A term
t
is
\mathsf{r}
-
normal
if there is no
u
such that
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}u
.
An evaluation
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}u
is
\mathsf{r}
-normalizing
if
u
is
\mathsf{r}
-normal.
A term
t
is
weakly
\mathsf{r}
-normalizing
if there is a
\mathsf{r}
-normalizing evaluation
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}u
; and
t
is
strongly
\mathsf{r}
-normalizing
if there no infinite sequence
(t_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
t_{0}=t
and
t_{i}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}t_{i+1}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Clearly, strong
\mathsf{r}
-normalization implies weak
\mathsf{r}
-normalization.

A relation
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
is
diamond
if
u_{1}\,{}_{\mathsf{r}}\!\!\;\mbox{}{\leftarrow}\ t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}u_{2}
and
u_{1}\neq u_{2}
imply
u_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}s\,{}_{\mathsf{r}}\!\!\;\mbox{}{\leftarrow}\ u_{2}
for some
s
.
As a consequence:

1.

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
is confluent (
i.e.

u_{1}\,{}_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}\!\!\;\mbox{}{\leftarrow}\ t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}u_{2}
implies
u_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}s\,{}_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}\!\!\;\mbox{}{\leftarrow}\ u_{2}
for some
s
);

2.

any term
t
has at most one normal form (
i.e.
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}s
with
u
and
s

\mathsf{r}
-normal, then
u=s
);

3.

all
\mathsf{r}
-evaluations with the same start and end terms have the same length (
i.e.
if
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}u
and
d^{\prime}\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}u
then
|d|=|d^{\prime}|
);

4.

t
is weakly
\mathsf{r}
-normalizing iff it is strongly
\mathsf{r}
-normalizing.

Two relations
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}_{1}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}_{2}}

strongly commute
if
u_{1}\,{}_{\mathsf{r}_{1}}\!\!\!\;\mbox{}{\leftarrow}\ t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}_{2}}u_{2}
implies
u_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}_{2}}s\,{}_{\mathsf{r}_{2}}\!\!\!\;\mbox{}{\leftarrow}\ u_{2}
for some
s
.
If
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}_{1}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}_{2}}
strongly commute and are diamond, then

1.

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}\,=\,\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}_{1}}\!\cup\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}_{2}}
is diamond,

2.

all
\mathsf{r}
-evaluations with the same start and end terms have the same number of any kind of steps (
i.e.
if
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}u
and
d^{\prime}\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}u
then
|d|_{\mathsf{r}_{1}}=|d^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{r}_{1}}
and
|d|_{\mathsf{r}_{2}}=|d^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{r}_{2}}
).

It is a strong form of confluence and implies
uniform normalization
(if there is a normalizing sequence from
t
then there are no diverging sequences from
t
) and the

random descent property
(all normalizing sequences from
t
have the same length)

Appendix B
Proofs of
Section

II

Lemma B.1

(Basic Properties of
\lambda_{\mathsf{vsc}}
)
.

1.

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{m}}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}
are strongly normalizing (separately).

2.

\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
are diamond (separately).

3.

\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
strongly commute.

Proof.

The statements of Lemma
B.1
are a refinement of some results proved in
[
10
]
, where
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
is denoted by
\to_{\mathsf{w}}
.

1.

See
[
10
, Lemma 3]
.

2.

We prove that
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
is diamond,
i.e.
if
u\;\mbox{}_{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ t\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}s
with
u\neq s
then there exists
t^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathsf{vsc}}
such that
u\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
.
The proof is by induction on the definition of
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
.
Since there
t\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}s\neq u
and the reduction
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
is weak, there are only eight cases:

•

Step at the Root for
t\!\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\!u
and Application Right for
t\!\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\!s
,
i.e.

t\coloneqq L\langle\lambda x.p\rangle q\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}L\langle p[x{\shortleftarrow}q]\rangle\eqqcolon u
and
t\!\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}\!L\langle\lambda x.p\rangle q^{\prime}\!\eqqcolon s
with
q\!\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\!q^{\prime}
: then,
u\!\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\!L\langle p[x{\shortleftarrow}q^{\prime}]\rangle\!\;\mbox{}_{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ \!s
;

•

Step at the Root for
t\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}u
and Application Left for
t\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}s
,
i.e.
, for some
n>0
,

t\coloneqq(\lambda x.p)[x_{1}{\shortleftarrow}t_{1}]\dots[x_{n}{\shortleftarrow}t_{n}]q\allowbreak\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}p[x{\shortleftarrow}q][x_{1}{\shortleftarrow}t_{1}]\dots[x_{n}{\shortleftarrow}t_{n}]\eqqcolon u

whereas
t\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\allowbreak(\lambda x.p)[x_{1}{\shortleftarrow}t_{1}]\dots[x_{j}{\shortleftarrow}t^{\prime}_{j}]\dots[x_{n}{\shortleftarrow}t_{n}]q\eqqcolon s
with

for some
1\leq j\leq n
: then,

\displaystyle u\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\allowbreak p[x{\shortleftarrow}q][x_{1}{\shortleftarrow}t_{1}]\dots[x_{j}{\shortleftarrow}t^{\prime}_{j}]\dots[x_{n}{\shortleftarrow}t_{n}]\;\mbox{}_{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ s;

•

Application Left for
t\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}u
and Application Right for
t\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}s
,

: then,

;

•

t\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}s
,

such that

, hence
u\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}q_{0}p\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{m}}{\leftarrow}\ s

\mathsf{ES}

We prove that
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
is diamond,

if

Note that in
[
10
, Lemma 11]
it has just been proved the strong confluence of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{m}}
or
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}
.

3.

We show that
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
and
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
strongly commute,
i.e.
if

, then
u\neq s
and there is
t^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathsf{vsc}}
such that
u\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{{{\mathsf{o}}{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
.
The proof is by induction on the definition of
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}u
.
The proof that
u\neq s
is left to the reader.
Since the
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{m}}
cannot reduce under
\lambda
’s, all values are
{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}
-normal and
{{{\mathsf{o}}{\mathsf{e}}}}
-normal. So, there are the following cases.

•

and

with

and
\mathsf{ES}
right for

,
i.e.

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\coloneqq q[z{\shortleftarrow}v[x_{1}{\shortleftarrow}t_{1}]\dots[x_{n}{\shortleftarrow}t_{n}]]

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}q\{z{\shortleftarrow}v\}[x_{1}{\shortleftarrow}t_{1}]\dots[x_{n}{\shortleftarrow}t_{n}]\eqqcolon u

and

Proposition B.2

(Properties of the open reduction)
.
†
†
margin:

See p.
II.1

proposition

II.1

1.

The reduction
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
is diamond.

2.

A term is
{\mathsf{o}}
-normal if and only if it is a fireball, where
fireballs
(and
inert terms
) are defined by:

Inert term

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\Coloneqq x\mid if\mid i[x{\shortleftarrow}i^{\prime}]

Fireball

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\Coloneqq v\mid i\mid f[x{\shortleftarrow}i]

Proof.

1.

Strong commutation of
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
is proven in Lemma
B.1
.
3
.
Diamond of
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
follows from that, from diamond for
\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}
and
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
(Lemma
B.1
.
2
),
and from Hindley-Rosen lemma (
[
13
, Prop. 3.3.5]
).

2.

See
[
10
, Lemma 5]
, where
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
is denoted by
\to_{\mathsf{w}}
.
∎

Lemma B.3

(Sizes for inert terms)
.

For every inert term
i
,
|i|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=|i|_{{\mathsf{s}}}
.

Proof.

By induction of the inert term
i
.
Cases:

•

Variable
,
i.e.

i=x
.
Then,
|i|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=0=|i|_{{\mathsf{s}}}
.

•

Application
,
i.e.

i=i^{\prime}f
.
By
i.h.
,
|i^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=|i^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}
.
Hence,
|i|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=1+|i^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{o}}}+|f|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=1+|i^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}+|f|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=|i|_{{\mathsf{s}}}
.

•

Explicit substitution
,
i.e.

i=i^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}i^{\prime\prime}]
.
By
i.h.
,
|i^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=|i^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}
and
|i^{\prime\prime}|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=|i^{\prime\prime}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}
.
Thus,
|i|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=|i^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{o}}}+|i^{\prime\prime}|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=|i^{\prime}|_{{\mathsf{s}}}+|i^{\prime\prime}|_{{\mathsf{o}}}=|i|_{{\mathsf{s}}}
.
∎

Lemma B.4

(Shape of strong fireballs)
.

Let
t
be a
strong
fireball. Then exactly one of the following holds:

•

either
t
is a
strong
inert term,

•

or
t
is a
strong
value.

Proof.

Proving that at least one of the two holds is left to the reader.
We now prove that only one of them holds:

•

Let
t
be a
strong
inert term. We prove that
t
is not a
strong
value by structural induction on
t
:

–

Variable
: Trivial.

–

Application
: Trivial.

–

\mathsf{ES}
;
i.e.
,
t=i_{\mathsf{s}}[x{\shortleftarrow}i^{\prime}_{\mathsf{s}}]
: Then
i_{\mathsf{s}}
is not a
strong
value—by
i.h.
—, and so neither is
t
.

•

Let
t=L\langle\lambda x.{f_{\mathsf{s}}}\rangle
, with
L=[x_{1}{\shortleftarrow}{i_{\mathsf{s}}}_{1}]\dots[x_{n}{\shortleftarrow}{i_{\mathsf{s}}}_{n}]
, with
n\geq 0
.
We prove that
t
is not a
strong
inert term by induction on
n
:

–

Empty substitution context
;
i.e.
,
L=\langle\cdot\rangle
: Trivial.

–

Non-empty substitution context
;
i.e.
,
L=L^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}{i_{\mathsf{s}}}_{n+1}]
: As
L^{\prime}\!\langle\lambda x.{f}\rangle
is not a
strong
inert term by
i.h.
, then neither is
L^{\prime}\!\langle\lambda x.{f}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}{i_{\mathsf{s}}}_{n+1}]=t
.
∎

Proposition B.5

(Properties of the
strong
reduction)
.
†
†
margin:

See p.
II.2

proposition

II.2

1.

The reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
is confluent.

2.

A term is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal if and only if it is a
strong
fireball, where
strong
fireballs
(and
strong
inert terms
,
strong
values
) are:

Proof.

1.

See
[
10
, Corollary 1]
.

2.

We prove the two directions of the equivalence separately.

(a)

Let
t
be
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal. We shall prove that
t
is a
strong
fireball by induction on
t
:

•

Variable
. Trivial.

•

Abstraction
;
i.e.
,
t=\lambda x.{u}
.
As
t
is
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}_{\lambda}}
-normal, so is
u
.
By
i.h.
,
u
is a
strong
fireball, and then so is
t
.

•

Application
;
i.e.
,
t=t_{1}t_{2}
.
Since
t
is
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}_{\lambda}}
-normal, so are
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
By
i.h.
,
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are
strong
fireballs.
Note that
t_{1}\neq L\langle\lambda x.{u}\rangle
, otherwise
t\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}L\langle u[x{\shortleftarrow}t_{2}]\rangle
which contradicts
\mathsf{vsc}
-normality of
t
.
So,
t_{1}
is a
strong
inert term (by
Lemma

B.4
), thus
t
is a
strong
fireball.

•

Explicit substitution
;
i.e.
,
t=t_{1}[x{\shortleftarrow}t_{2}]
.
Since
t
is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal, then so are
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
By
i.h.
,
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are
strong
fireballs.
Note that
t_{2}\neq L\langle v\rangle
, otherwise
t\mapsto_{{\mathsf{e}}}L\langle t_{1}\{x{\shortleftarrow}v\}\rangle
which contradicts the
\mathsf{vsc}
-normality of
t
.
Thus
t_{2}
is a
strong
inert term (by
Lemma

B.4
), and so
t
is a
strong
fireball.

(b)

Let
t
be a
strong
fireball.
We prove that
t
is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal by induction on the definition of
strong
fireball.

•

Variable
. Trivial.

•

Abstraction
;
i.e.
,
t=\lambda x.{f_{\mathsf{s}}}
.
By
i.h.
,
f_{\mathsf{s}}
is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal, and hence so is
t
.

•

Application
;
i.e.
,
t=i_{\mathsf{s}}f_{\mathsf{s}}
.
By
i.h.
,
i_{\mathsf{s}}
and
f_{\mathsf{s}}
are
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal.
Since
i_{\mathsf{s}}
is not of the of the form
L\langle\lambda x.{u}\rangle
(by
Lemma

B.4
), then
t
is also
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal.

•

Explicit substitution
;
i.e.
,
t=f_{\mathsf{s}}[x{\shortleftarrow}i_{\mathsf{s}}]
(it includes the case when
f_{\mathsf{s}}
is a
strong
inert term).
By
i.h.
, both
f_{\mathsf{s}}
and
i_{\mathsf{s}}
are
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal.
Since
i_{\mathsf{s}}
is not of the form
L\langle v\rangle
(by Lemma
B.4
), then
t
is also
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal.
∎

Lemma B.6

(Strong normalization of
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
)
.

The reduction
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
is strongly normalizing.

Proof.

Each
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
step strictly decreases the number of
ES
. This shows that
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
is strongly normalizing.
A proof that
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
is strongly normalizing is in
[
10
, Lemma 3]
, where it is denoted
\to_{\mathsf{vs}}
.
∎

Lemma B.7

(Preservation of normal forms)
.

Let
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}u
. Then,
t
is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal if and only if
u
is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal.

Proof.

By induction on the definition of
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}u
.
∎

Lemma B.8

(Swaps)
.

1.

\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{m}}\,\subseteq\,\rightarrow_{\mathsf{m}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
;

2.

\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\,\subseteq\,\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}\!\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}
;

3.

\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{+}\,\subseteq\,\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{+}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}
.

Proof.

The only non trivial point is the third one, that we now prove. The hypothesis is
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{k}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{h}u
for some
k,h
. The proof is by lexicographic induction on
(n,k)
, where
n
is the length of the longest
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
sequence from
t
(it exists because
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
is strongly normalizing,
Lemma

B.6
). Now the case
k=h=1
is given by the second point. Then consider
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{k}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{h}u
, and apply the swap of the second point to the central pair. There are two cases:

1.

\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\subseteq\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}
, and so
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{k}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{h}u
.
Now by
i.h.
(on
k
) applied to the prefix
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{k}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}
we obtain
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{+}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{h}u
.
By
i.h.
(on the longest sequence), the suffix
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{h}
turns into
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{+}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}
, giving
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{+}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{+}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}u
, that is, the statement holds.

2.

\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\subseteq\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}
, and so
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{k}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{h}u
.
By
i.h.
(on
k
) applied to the whole sequence,
t\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}^{+}\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}u
.
∎

Proposition B.9

(Irrelevance of
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
)
.
†
†
margin:

See p.
II.3

proposition

II.3

1.

Postponement
: whenever
d\colon t\,(\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}})^{*}\,u
then there is
d^{\prime}\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}\!\cdot\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}\,u
with
|d^{\prime}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|d|_{\mathsf{m}}
;

2.

Termination
: a term is weakly (
resp.
strongly) normalizing for
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
if and only if so is it for
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\!\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
.

Proof.

•

Postponement
: By induction on the length
|d|
of the evaluation
d:t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}u
, using Lemma
B.8
.

•

Termination:
Clearly, if
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
is strongly normalizing on
t
then
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
is strongly normalizing on
t
, since
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\,\subseteq\,\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
.

Conversely, suppose that
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
is not strongly normalizing on
t
.
Then, there is an infinite sequence of terms
(t_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
t_{i}\,(\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}})\,t_{i+1}
and
t=t_{0}
.
Hence, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is an evaluation
d_{n}\colon t\,(\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}})^{*}\,t_{n}
with
|d_{n}|_{\mathsf{m}}=n
and, by postponement irrelevance (
Item

1
), there is an evaluation
d^{\prime}_{n}\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}u_{n}
with
|d^{\prime}_{n}|_{\mathsf{m}}=n
.
Thus
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
is not strongly normalizing on
t
(by Konig’s lemma).

If
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
is weakly normalizing on
t
then
t\,(\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}})^{*}\,u
for some
u
normal for
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
.
By postponement irrelevance (
Item

1
),
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}s\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}u
where
s
is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal by
lemma

B.7
.
Therefore,
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
is weakly normalizing on
t
.

If
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
is weakly normalizing on
t
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}s
for some
s
that is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal.
Since
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
is strongly normalizing (
lemma

B.6
),
s\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}u
for some
u
that is
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
-normal.
By
lemma

B.7
,
u
is also
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal.
Therefore,
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}
is weakly normalizing on
t
.
∎

Proposition B.10

(Simulation)
.
†
†
margin:

See p.
II.4

proposition

II.4

Let
t
be a term without
ES
.

1.

If
t\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}t^{\prime}
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{m}}\!\cdot\,(\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}})\ t^{\prime}
.

2.

If
t\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}^{*}t^{\prime}
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}u\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}t^{\prime}
for some term
u
.

Proof.

1.

By induction on the context
C
such that
t=C\langle s\rangle\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}C\langle s^{\prime}\rangle=t^{\prime}
with
s\mapsto_{\beta_{v}}s^{\prime}
.

If
C=\langle\cdot\rangle
, then we have the root-step
t=(\lambda x.s)v_{\mathsf{t}}\mapsto_{\beta_{v}}s\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}=t^{\prime}
.
Then,
t\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}s[x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}]\mapsto_{{\mathsf{e}}}s\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}=t^{\prime}
.

The cases where
C\neq\langle\cdot\rangle
follow from the
i.h.
easily.

2.

Let
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}^{*}t^{\prime}
. We prove that
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}u\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}t^{\prime}
for some term
u
, by induction on
|d|_{\beta_{v}}\!\in\mathbb{N}
.

If
n=0
then
t=t^{\prime}
and we are done taking
u\coloneqq t
.

Otherwise,
n>0
and
t\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}s\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}^{*}t^{\prime}
for some term
s
without
ES
.
By
i.h.
,
s\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}t^{\prime}
.
According to Point
1
,
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{m}}\!\cdot\,(\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}})\ s
.
Therefore,
t\ (\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}\cup\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}})^{*}\ t^{\prime}
and so, by
Proposition

II.3
.
1
,
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}^{*}u\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{var}}}^{*}t^{\prime}
for some term
u
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs of
Section

III

Note that rigid terms are not abstractions.

Remark C.1
.

Every
strong
inert term is a rigid term, but the converse does not hold (take
y\,\lambda x.{\delta\delta}
).

Lemma C.1

(Properties of rigid terms)
.

1.

For any term
t
and any rigid context
R
,
R\langle t\rangle
is a rigid term.

2.

Let
r
be a rigid term and
t
be a term such that
r\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}t
. Then
t
is rigid.

3.

Let
r
be a rigid term and
t
be a term such that
r\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}t
. Then
t
is rigid.

4.

Let
t
be a term that is
{\mathsf{o}}
-normal and is not an
answer
. Then
t
is rigid.

5.

Let
r
be a rigid term that is not
\mathsf{x}
-normal.
Then there exist a rigid context
R
and terms
u,u^{\prime}
such that
r=R\langle u\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}R\langle u^{\prime}\rangle
with
u\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}u^{\prime}
.

Proof.

1.

By induction on the definition of
R
.

2.

Let
O
be an open evaluation context such that
r=O\langle u\rangle\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}O\langle u^{\prime}\rangle=t
, with
u\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}u^{\prime}
or
u\mapsto_{{\mathsf{e}}}u^{\prime}
. We proceed by induction on the open context
O
.

•

Empty context
;
i.e.
,
O=\langle\cdot\rangle
.
This case is not possible, because it would imply that

–

either
r=u=L\langle\lambda x.{s}\rangle q
(if
u\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}u^{\prime}
),

–

or
r=u={s}[x{\shortleftarrow}L\langle v\rangle]
(if
u\mapsto_{{\mathsf{e}}}u^{\prime}
),

and in both cases
r
is not a rigid term.

•

Application right
;
i.e.
,
O=qO^{\prime}
.
As
r=qO^{\prime}\langle u\rangle
, then
q
is a rigid term, and so
qO^{\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle=t
is rigid.

•

Application left
;
i.e.
,
O=O^{\prime}q
.
As
r=O^{\prime}\langle u\rangle q
and
O^{\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle
is rigid by
i.h.
, then
O^{\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle q=t
is rigid too.

•

\mathsf{ES}
left
;
i.e.
,
O=O^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}q]
. Since
r=O^{\prime}\langle u\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}q]
, then both
O^{\prime}\langle u\rangle
and
q
are rigid. Moreover,
O^{\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle
is rigid by
i.h.
, and so
O^{\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}q]=t
is rigid too.

•

\mathsf{ES}
right
;
i.e.
,
O=q[x{\shortleftarrow}O^{\prime}]
. Since
r=q[x{\shortleftarrow}O^{\prime}\langle u\rangle]
, then both
q
and
O^{\prime}\langle u\rangle
are rigid. Moreover,
O^{\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle
is rigid by
i.h.
, and so
q[x{\shortleftarrow}O^{\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle]=t
is rigid too.

3.

Let
S
be a strong evaluation context such that
r=S\langle u\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}S\langle u^{\prime}\rangle=t
, with
u\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}u^{\prime}
or
u\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}u^{\prime}
. We proceed by induction on the external context
S
.

•

Empty context
;
i.e.
,
S=\langle\cdot\rangle
. Then
r=u\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}u^{\prime}=t
or
r=u\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}{{\mathsf{e}}}}u^{\prime}=t
, and the statement holds by Lemma
C.1
.
2

•

Under
\lambda
-abstraction right
;
i.e.
,
S=\lambda x.{S^{\prime}}
. This case is not possible, because it would imply that
r=\lambda x.{S^{\prime}\langle u\rangle}
, which is not a rigid term.

•

Strong context,
\mathsf{ES}
right
;
i.e.
,
S=q[x{\shortleftarrow}R]
. Since
r=q[x{\shortleftarrow}R\langle u\rangle]
, then both
q
and
R\langle u\rangle
are rigid terms. Moreover,
R\langle u^{\prime}\rangle
is rigid by
i.h.
, and so
q[x{\shortleftarrow}R\langle u^{\prime}\rangle]=s
is rigid too.

•

Strong context,
\mathsf{ES}
left
;
i.e.
,
S=S^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}q]
, with
q
a rigid term. Since
r=S^{\prime}\langle u\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}q]
, then
S^{\prime}\langle u\rangle
is rigid. Moreover,
S^{\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle
is rigid by
i.h.
, and so
S^{\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}q]=s
is rigid too.

•

Rigid context, application right
;
i.e.
,
S=qS^{\prime}
, with
q
a rigid term. Then
qS^{\prime}\langle u^{\prime}\rangle=s
is rigid too.

•

Rigid context, application left
;
i.e.
,
S=Rq
. Since
r=R\langle u\rangle q
, then
R\langle u\rangle
is rigid. Moreover,
R\langle u^{\prime}\rangle
is rigid by
i.h.
, and so
R\langle u^{\prime}\rangle q
is rigid too.

•

Rigid context,
\mathsf{ES}
left
;
i.e.
,
S=R[x{\shortleftarrow}q]
, with
q
a rigid term. Since
r=R\langle u\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}q]
, then
R\langle u\rangle
is rigid. Moreover,
R\langle u^{\prime}\rangle
is rigid by
i.h.
, and so
R\langle u^{\prime}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}q]=s
is rigid too.

•

Rigid context,
\mathsf{ES}
right
;
i.e.
,
S=q[x{\shortleftarrow}R]
, with
q
a rigid term. Since
r=q[x{\shortleftarrow}R\langle u\rangle]
, then
R\langle u\rangle
is rigid. Moreover,
R\langle u^{\prime}\rangle
is rigid by
i.h.
, and so
q[x{\shortleftarrow}R\langle u^{\prime}\rangle]=s
is rigid too.

4.

By structural induction on the term
t
. Cases:

•

Variable
. Trivial.

•

Abstraction
. The statement is vacuously true, because if
t
is an abstraction then it is an
answer
.

•

Application
;
i.e.
,
t=t_{1}t_{2}
. If
t_{1}
is not in
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
-normal form, then neither is
t
. Moreover, if
t_{1}
is a
\lambda
-abstraction in a substitution context, then
t
is not in
\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}
-normal form, which is absurd. Thus,
t_{1}
is rigid—by
i.h.
—and then so is
t
.

•

\mathsf{ES}
;
i.e.
,
t=t_{1}[x{\shortleftarrow}t_{2}]
. First of all, if
t_{1}
is a
\lambda
-abstraction in a substitution context then so is
t
—which is absurd—, and if
t_{1}
is not in
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
-normal form then neither is
t
. Therefore,
t_{1}
is a rigid term —by
i.h.
. Second, if
t_{2}
is an abstraction in a substitution context then
t
is not in
\mapsto_{{\mathsf{e}}}
-normal form —which is absurd—, and if
t_{2}
is not in
\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}
-normal form then neither is
t
. Therefore,
t_{2}
is a rigid term —by
i.h.
. Thus,
t
is a rigid term as well.

5.

By induction on the rigid term
r
. Cases:

•

Variable
. The statement is vacuously true, because if
r
is a variable then it is
\mathsf{x}
-normal.

•

Application
;
i.e.
,
t=t_{1}t_{2}
, with
t_{1}
a rigid term. Note that
t_{1}
is not an
answer
.

Let
t_{1}
be not in
\mathsf{x}
-normal form. Then by
i.h.
there exist a rigid context
R^{\prime}
and terms
s,s^{\prime}
such that
t_{1}=R^{\prime\prime}\langle u\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}R^{\prime}\langle s^{\prime}\rangle
. Thus, the statement holds by taking
R\coloneqq R^{\prime}t_{2}
,
u\coloneqq s
and
u^{\prime}\coloneqq s^{\prime}
.

Let
t_{1}
be in
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
-normal form. Then
t_{2}
is not
\mathsf{x}
-normal, which implies the existence of a external context
S
and terms
s,s^{\prime}
such that
t_{2}=S\langle s\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}S\langle s^{\prime}\rangle
and
s\rightarrow_{{\mathsf{o}}}s^{\prime}
, and so the statement holds by taking
R\coloneqq t_{1}S
.

•

\mathsf{ES}
;
i.e.
,
t=t_{1}[x{\shortleftarrow}t_{2}]
, with both
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
rigid terms. If
t_{1}
is not
\mathsf{x}
-normal, then there exist—by
i.h.
—a rigid context
R^{\prime}
and terms
s,s^{\prime}
such that
t_{1}=R^{\prime}\langle s\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}R^{\prime}\langle s^{\prime}\rangle
. Thus, the statement holds by taking
R\coloneqq R^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}t_{2}]
,
u\coloneqq s
and
u^{\prime}\coloneqq s^{\prime}
.

If
t_{1}
is in
\mathsf{x}
-normal form, then
t_{2}
is not in
\mathsf{x}
-normal form and so there exist rigid context
R^{\prime}
and terms
s,s^{\prime}
such that
t_{2}=R^{\prime}\langle s\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}R^{\prime}\langle s^{\prime}\rangle
. Thus, the statement holds by taking
R\coloneqq t_{1}[x{\shortleftarrow}R^{\prime}]
,
u\coloneqq s
and
u^{\prime}\coloneqq s^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma C.2

(Basic Properties of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
)
.

1.

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
are diamond (separately).

2.

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
strongly commute.

Proof.

1.

We prove that
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
is diamond,
i.e.
if
u\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
with
u\neq s
then there exists a term
t^{\prime}
such that
u\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
(the proof that
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
is diamond is analogue).
The proof is by structural induction on
t
, doing case analysis on
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
:

•

Under
\lambda
-abstraction for both
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;
i.e.
,
t=\lambda x.{q}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}\lambda x.{p}=u
and
t=\lambda x.{q}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}\lambda x.{s}=s
,
with
q\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}p
and
q\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
.
By
i.h.
there exists
q^{\prime}
such that
p\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
and so
u=\lambda x.{p}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}\lambda x.{q^{\prime}}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ \lambda x.{s}=s
.

•

Application right for
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and application left for
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;
i.e.
,
t=qp\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}qp^{\prime}=u
and
t=qp\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q^{\prime}p=s
. There are several sub-cases to this:

–

Let
t=qp=qO\langle\tilde{p}\rangle\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}qO\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle=u
, with
\tilde{p}\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{p^{\prime}}
, and
t=R\langle\tilde{q}\rangle p\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle p
, with
\tilde{q}\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\tilde{q^{\prime}}
. Let
t^{\prime}\coloneqq R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle O\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle
, having that

u=R\langle\tilde{q}\rangle O\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle O\langle\tilde{p}\rangle=s

Note that
u\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}
holds because every rigid context is an open context.

–

Let
t=qp=qO_{1}\langle\tilde{p}\rangle\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}qO_{1}\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle=u
, with
\tilde{p}\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{p^{\prime}}
, and
t=O_{2}\langle\tilde{q}\rangle p\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}O_{2}\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle p
, with
\tilde{q}\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{q^{\prime}}
.
Then the statement holds by Lemma
B.1
.
2

–

Let
t=qS\langle\tilde{p}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}qS\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle=u
, with
q
a rigid term and
\tilde{p}\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\tilde{p^{\prime}}
, and
t=R\langle\tilde{q}\rangle p\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle p=s
, with
\tilde{q}\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\tilde{q^{\prime}}
. Let
t^{\prime}\coloneqq R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle
, having that

u=R\langle\tilde{q}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p}\rangle=s

Note that
t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
holds because
R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle
is a rigid term —by Lemma
C.1
.
1
.

–

Let
t=qS\langle\tilde{p}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}qS\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle=u
, with
q
a rigid term and
\tilde{p}\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\tilde{p^{\prime}}
, and
t=O\langle\tilde{q}\rangle p\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}O\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle p
, with
\tilde{q}\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{q^{\prime}}
. Let
t^{\prime}\coloneqq O\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle
, having that

u=O\langle\tilde{q}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ O\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p}\rangle=s

Note that
t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
holds because the fact that
q
is a rigid term and that
q=O\langle\tilde{q}\rangle\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}O\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle
imply that
O\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle
is a rigid term —by Lemma
C.1
.
2
.

•

Application right for both
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;
i.e.
,
t=qp\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}qp^{\prime}=u
and
t=qp\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}qp^{\prime\prime}=s
. By
i.h.
there exists a term
s
such that
p^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ p^{\prime\prime}
. The analysis of the sub-cases, depending on the open/strong/rigid type contexts involved in
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
, follows the same schema as for the previous item, all showing that

u=qp^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}qs\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ qp^{\prime\prime}=s

•

Application left for both
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;
i.e.
,
t=qp\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q^{\prime}p=u
and
t=qp\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q^{\prime\prime}p=s
. By
i.h.
there exists a term
s
such that
q^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ q^{\prime\prime}
. The analysis of the sub-cases, depending on the open/strong/rigid type contexts involved in
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
, follows the same schema as for the previous item, all showing that

u=q^{\prime}p\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}sp\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ q^{\prime\prime}p=s

•

\mathsf{ES}
right for
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and
\mathsf{ES}
left for
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;
i.e.
,
t=q[x{\shortleftarrow}p]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q[x{\shortleftarrow}p^{\prime}]=u
and
t=q[x{\shortleftarrow}p]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}p]
. There are several sub-cases to this:

–

Let
t=q[x{\shortleftarrow}O\langle\tilde{p}\rangle]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q[x{\shortleftarrow}O\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle]=u
, with
\tilde{p}\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{p^{\prime}}
, and
t=O\langle\tilde{q}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}p]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}O\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}p]=s
, with
\tilde{q}\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{q^{\prime}}
. Then the statement holds by Lemma
B.1
.
2
.

–

Let
t=q[x{\shortleftarrow}O\langle\tilde{p}\rangle]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q[x{\shortleftarrow}O\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle]=u
, with
\tilde{p}\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{p^{\prime}}
, and
t=S\langle\tilde{q}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}p]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}S\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}p]=s
, with
\tilde{q}\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\tilde{q^{\prime}}
and
p
is a rigid term. Let
t^{\prime}\coloneqq S\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}O\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle]
, having that

u=S\langle\tilde{q}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}O\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ S\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}O\langle\tilde{p}\rangle]=s

Note that
u\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}
holds because the fact that
p
is a rigid term and that
p=O\langle\tilde{p}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}O\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle
imply that

C.1
.

and

\mathsf{ES}
right for both
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;

. By

s
such that

. The analysis of the sub-cases, depending on the open/strong/rigid type contexts involved in

and

, follows the same schema as for the previous item, all showing that

u=q[x{\shortleftarrow}p^{\prime}]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ q[x{\shortleftarrow}p^{\prime\prime}]=s

•

\mathsf{ES}
left for both
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;
i.e.
,
t=q[x{\shortleftarrow}p]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}p]=u
and
t=q^{\prime\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}p]
. By
i.h.
there exists a term
s
such that
q^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ q^{\prime\prime}
. The analysis of the sub-cases, depending on the open/strong/rigid type contexts involved in
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
, follows the same schema as for the previous item, all showing that

u=q^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}p]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s[x{\shortleftarrow}p]\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}{\leftarrow}\ q^{\prime\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}p]=s

The proof that
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
is diamond (
i.e.
, if
u\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}s
with
u\neq s
then there exists
t^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathsf{vsc}}
such that
u\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
) follows the same schema as for
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
.

2.

We show that
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
strongly commute;
i.e.
, if
u\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
, then
u\neq s
and there is
t^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathsf{vsc}}
such that
u\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
. The proof is by structural induction on
t
, doing case analysis on
t\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
:

•

Under
\lambda
-abstraction for both
t\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ u
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;
i.e.
,
t=\lambda x.{p}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ \lambda x.{q}=u
and
t=\lambda x.{q}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}\lambda x.{s}=s
, with
p\;\mbox{}_{{{{\mathsf{o}}{\mathsf{e}}}}}{\leftarrow}\ q\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}s
. By Lemma
B.1
.
3
, there exists
q^{\prime}
such that
p\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
, and so
u=\lambda x.{p}\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\lambda x.{q^{\prime}}\;\mbox{}_{{{{\mathsf{o}}{\mathsf{e}}}}}{\leftarrow}\ \lambda x.{s}
.

•

Application right for
u\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ t
and application left for
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;
i.e.
,
u=qp^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ qp=t
and
t=qp\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}q^{\prime}p=s
. There are several sub-cases to this:

–

Let
u=qO\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ qO\langle\tilde{p}\rangle=t
, with
\tilde{p^{\prime}}\;\mbox{}_{{{{\mathsf{o}}{\mathsf{e}}}}}{\leftarrow}\ \tilde{p}
, and
t=R\langle\tilde{q}\rangle p\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle p=s
, with
\tilde{q}\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\tilde{q^{\prime}}
. Let
t^{\prime}=R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle O\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle
, having that

u=R\langle\tilde{q}\rangle O\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle O\langle\tilde{p}\rangle=s

–

Let
u=qO_{1}\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ qO_{1}\langle\tilde{p}\rangle=t
, and
t=O_{2}\langle\tilde{q}\rangle p\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}O_{2}\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle p=s
, with
\tilde{q}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}\tilde{q^{\prime}}
. Then the statement holds by Lemma
B.1
.
3

–

Let
u=qS\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ qS\langle\tilde{p}\rangle=t
, with
q
a rigid term and
\tilde{p^{\prime}}\;\mbox{}_{{{{\mathsf{o}}{\mathsf{e}}}}}{\leftarrow}\ \tilde{p}
, and
t=R\langle\tilde{q}\rangle p\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle p=s
, with
\tilde{q}\rightarrow_{{{{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{m}}}}\tilde{q^{\prime}}
. Let
t^{\prime}=R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle
, having that

u=R\langle\tilde{q}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p}\rangle=s

Note that
t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
holds because
R\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle
is a rigid term —by Lemma
C.1
.
3
.

–

Let
u=qS\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ qS\langle\tilde{p}\rangle=t
, with
q
a rigid term and
\tilde{p^{\prime}}\;\mbox{}_{{{{\mathsf{o}}{\mathsf{e}}}}}{\leftarrow}\ \tilde{p}
, and
t=O\langle\tilde{q}\rangle p\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}O\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle p=s
, with
\tilde{q}\mapsto_{\mathsf{m}}\tilde{q^{\prime}}
. Let
t^{\prime}=O\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle
, having that

u=O\langle\tilde{q}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p^{\prime}}\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ O\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle S\langle\tilde{p}\rangle=s

Note that
t^{\prime}\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ s
holds because
O\langle\tilde{q^{\prime}}\rangle
is rigid—by Lemma
C.1
.
2
.

•

Application left for
u\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ t
and application right for
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;
i.e.
,
u=q^{\prime}p\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ qp=t
and

. There are several sub-cases to this:

–

, with
\tilde{q^{\prime}}\;\mbox{}_{{{{\mathsf{o}}{\mathsf{e}}}}}{\leftarrow}\ \tilde{q}

.

–

Let

, with

Application right for both

\mathsf{ES}

and

\mathsf{ES}
left for

and

there exists a term

and

\mathsf{ES}
left for both
u\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ t
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s
;

,

u=q^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}p]\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ q[x{\shortleftarrow}p]=t

and

By

there exists a term

such that
p^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}p\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ p^{\prime\prime}
. The analysis of the sub-cases, depending on the open / strong / rigid type contexts involved in
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}u
and

, follows the same schema as for the previous item, all showing that

u=q^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}p]\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}s[x{\shortleftarrow}p]\;\mbox{}_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}{\leftarrow}\ q^{\prime\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}p]=s.\qed

Proposition C.3

(Properties of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
)
.
†
†
margin:

See p.
III.1

proposition

III.1

Let
t
be a VSC term.

1.

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
is diamond;
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
strongly commute.

2.

Fullness
:

t
is
\mathsf{x}
-normal if and only if
t
is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal.

Proof.

1.

Strong commutation of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
is proven in Lemma
C.2
.
2
.
Diamond of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}
follows immediately from that, from diamond for
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
(Lemma
C.2
.
1
),
and from Hindley-Rosen lemma (
[
13
, Prop. 3.3.5]
).

2.

If
t
is
\mathsf{vsc}
-normal then it is
\mathsf{x}
-normal because
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}}\,\subseteq\,\rightarrow_{\mathsf{vsc}}
.

Conversely, suppose that
t
is
\mathsf{x}
-normal. By
Proposition

II.2
.
2
, it is enough to show that
t
is a
strong
fireball.
To have the right
i.h.
, we prove simultaneously, by induction on
t
, the following stronger statements (we recall that all
strong
inert terms are
strong
fireballs):

(a)

Fireball property
: If
t
is
\mathsf{x}
-normal, then
t
is a
strong
fireball.

(b)

Non-value property
: If
t
is
\mathsf{x}
-normal and not an
answer
, then
t
is a
strong
inert term.

Cases:

•

Variable
,
i.e.
,
t=x
: both properties trivially hold, since
t
is a
strong
inert term and so a
strong
fireball.

•

Abstraction
,
i.e.
,
t=\lambda x.{u}
:

(a)

Non-value property
: vacuously true, as
t
is an abstraction and hence an
answer
.

(b)

Fireball property
: Since
t
is
\mathsf{x}
-normal, so is
u
.
By
i.h.
applied to
u
(fireball property),
u
is a
strong
fireball and hence so is
t
(as a
strong
value).

•

Application
;
i.e.
,
t=t_{1}t_{2}
(which is not an
answer
):

(a)

Non-value property
: Since
t
is
\mathsf{x}
-normal, so are
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Moreover,
t_{1}
is not an
answer
(otherwise
t
would be a
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{\mathsf{m}}}
-redex).
By
i.h.
applied to
t_{1}
(non-value property) and to
t_{2}
(fireball property),

t_{1}
is a
strong
inert term and
t_{2}
is a
strong
fireball.
Thus,
t
is a
strong
inert term.

(b)

Fireball property
: We have just proved that
t
is a
strong
inert term, and hence it is a
strong
fireball.

•

Explicit substitutions
,
i.e.
,
t=t_{1}[x{\shortleftarrow}t_{2}]
:

(a)

Fireball property
: Since
t
is
\mathsf{x}
-normal, so are
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Moreover,
t_{2}
is not an
answer
(otherwise
t
would be a
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{x}{{\mathsf{e}}}}
-redex).
By
i.h.
applied to
t_{1}
(fireball property) and to
t_{2}
(non-value property),

t_{1}
is a
strong
fireball and
t_{2}
is a
strong
inert term.
Thus,
t
is a
strong
fireball.

(b)

Non-value property
: We have just proved that
t
is a
strong
fireball.
If moreover
t
is not a
answer
, then
t_{1}
is not an
answer
and hence, by
i.h.
applied to
t_{1}
(non-value property),
t_{1}
is a
strong
inert term.
So,
t
is a
strong
inert term.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs of
Section

IV

First, we observe the following property: given a derivation for a term
t
, all variables associated with a non-empty multi type in the type context are free variables of
t
.

Remark D.1
.

If
\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
then
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)\subseteq{\sf fv}(t)
.

Lemma D.1

(Typing of values: splitting)
.

Let
\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
(for
v_{\mathsf{t}}
theoretical value).

1.

If
{\mathsf{M}}=\mathbf{0}
, then
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
and

|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=0=|\Phi|
.

2.

For every splitting
{\mathsf{M}}={\mathsf{M}}_{1}\uplus{\mathsf{M}}_{2}
, there exist
type derivations
\Phi_{1}\vartriangleright{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}_{1}
and

\Phi_{2}\vartriangleright{\Gamma_{2}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}_{2}
such that
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\uplus\Gamma_{2}
,

|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi_{1}|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Phi_{2}|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Phi|=|\Phi_{1}|+|\Phi_{2}|
.

Proof.

1.

By a simple inspection of the typing rules,
{\mathsf{M}}=\mathbf{0}
and the fact that
v_{\mathsf{t}}
is a value imply
that

\Phi
is of the form

\mathsf{many}

{}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!\mathbf{0}

where
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
and
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=0=|\Phi|
.

2.

Let

\Phi=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 11.41953pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{many}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\left(\vrule height=27.69427pt,depth=1.5pt,width=0.0pt\right.$}$\left.\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 19.46007pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox to18.66656pt{\leaders{\vbox to3.44444pt{\vss\hbox{.}\vss\par}}{\vfill}\par}\hbox to0.0pt{ \vbox{\vskip 5.16663pt\hbox{\ignorespaces$\Phi_{i}$}\vskip 5.16663pt\par}\hss}}}\par}\par}}\hbox{${\Gamma_{i}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}_{i}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\right)_{{i\in I}}$}}}\hbox{${\biguplus_{{i\in I}}\Gamma_{i}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}]_{{i\in I}}$}}\par}\par}}}$}

with
\biguplus_{{i\in I}}\Gamma_{i}=\Gamma
and
[{\mathsf{A}}]_{{i\in I}}={\mathsf{M}}={\mathsf{M}}_{1}\uplus{\mathsf{M}}_{2}
. Let

I_{1}
and
I_{2}
be sets of indices such that
I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}
,
{\mathsf{M}}_{1}=[{\mathsf{A}}_{i}]_{i\in I_{1}}

and
{\mathsf{M}}_{2}=[{\mathsf{A}}_{i}]_{i\in I_{2}}
.
As
v_{\mathsf{t}}
is a value, we can then derive

\Phi_{1}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 15.32236pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{many}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\left(\vrule height=27.69427pt,depth=1.5pt,width=0.0pt\right.$}$\left.\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 19.46007pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox to18.66656pt{\leaders{\vbox to3.44444pt{\vss\hbox{.}\vss\par}}{\vfill}\par}\hbox to0.0pt{ \vbox{\vskip 5.16663pt\hbox{\ignorespaces$\Phi_{i}$}\vskip 5.16663pt\par}\hss}}}\par}\par}}\hbox{${\Gamma_{i}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}_{i}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\right)_{i\in I_{1}}$}}}\hbox{${\biguplus_{i\in I_{1}}\Gamma_{i}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}]_{i\in I_{1}}$}}\par}\par}}}$}

and

\Phi_{2}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 15.32236pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{many}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\left(\vrule height=27.69427pt,depth=1.5pt,width=0.0pt\right.$}$\left.\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 19.46007pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox to18.66656pt{\leaders{\vbox to3.44444pt{\vss\hbox{.}\vss\par}}{\vfill}\par}\hbox to0.0pt{ \vbox{\vskip 5.16663pt\hbox{\ignorespaces$\Phi_{i}$}\vskip 5.16663pt\par}\hss}}}\par}\par}}\hbox{${\Gamma_{i}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}_{i}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\right)_{i\in I_{2}}$}}}\hbox{${\biguplus_{i\in I_{2}}\Gamma_{i}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}]_{i\in I_{2}}$}}\par}\par}}}$}

noting that

\Gamma=\biguplus_{{i\in I}}\Gamma_{i}=\left(\biguplus_{i\in I_{1}}\Gamma_{i}\right)\uplus\left(\biguplus_{i\in I_{2}}\Gamma_{i}\right)

with

|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=\sum_{{i\in I}}|\Phi_{i}|_{\mathsf{m}}=\left(\sum_{i\in I_{1}}|\Phi_{i}|_{\mathsf{m}}\right)+\left(\sum_{i\in I_{2}}|\Phi_{i}|_{\mathsf{m}}\right)=|\Phi_{1}|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Phi_{2}|_{\mathsf{m}}

and

|\Phi|=\sum_{{i\in I}}|\Phi_{i}|=\left(\sum_{i\in I_{1}}|\Phi_{i}|\right)+\left(\sum_{i\in I_{2}}|\Phi_{i}|\right)=|\Phi_{1}|+|\Phi_{2}|

∎

Lemma D.2

(Substitution)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof p.
IV.2

Let
t
be a term,
v_{\mathsf{t}}
be a theoretical value and
\Phi\vartriangleright{\Gamma,x\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}}\vdash t\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
and
\Psi\vartriangleright{\Delta}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}
be derivations.
Then there is a derivation
\Theta\vartriangleright{\Gamma\uplus\Delta}\vdash t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}

with
|\Theta|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Theta|\leq|\Phi|+|\Psi|
.

Proof.

By induction on the term
t
.
Cases:

•

Variable
, then are two sub-cases:

1.

t=x
, then
t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}=x\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}=v_{\mathsf{t}}
and
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=0
and
|\Phi|=1
.

the derivation
\Phi
has necessarily the form (for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
)

\Phi=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 13.65279pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{many}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 27.26973pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{ax}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 26.31253pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{ax}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{${x\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}_{1}]}\vdash x\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}_{1}$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$\overset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\ldots}$}}\hbox{}}\hbox{${x\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]}\vdash x\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}_{n}$}}\par}\par}}}}}\hbox{${x\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]}\vdash x\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]$}}\par}\par}}}$}

with
{\mathsf{M}}=[{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]={\mathsf{N}}
and
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
.
Thus,
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=0
and
|\Phi|=n
.
Let
\Theta=\Psi
: so,
\Theta\vartriangleright{\Gamma\uplus\Delta}\vdash t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
(since
\Gamma\uplus\Delta=\Delta
) with
|\Theta|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Theta|=|\Psi|\leq|\Psi|+|\Phi|
(note that
|\Theta|=|\Phi|+|\Psi|
if and only if
n=0
).

2.

t=z\neq x
, then
t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}=z
and

|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=0
,
|\Phi|=1
,
the derivation
\Phi
has necessarily the form (for some
n~\in~\mathbb{N}
)

\Phi=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 13.65279pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{many}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 26.64476pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{ax}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 25.68756pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$\mathsf{ax}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{${z\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}_{1}]}\vdash z\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}_{1}$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$\overset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\ldots}$}}\hbox{}}\hbox{${z\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]}\vdash z\!:\!{\mathsf{A}}_{n}$}}\par}\par}}}}}\hbox{${z\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]}\vdash z\!:\![{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]$}}\par}\par}}}$}

where
{\mathsf{M}}=[{\mathsf{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{A}}_{n}]
and
{\mathsf{N}}=\mathbf{0}
and
\Gamma=z\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
(while
\Gamma(x)=\mathbf{0}
).
Thus,
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=0
and
|\Phi|=n
.
By Lemma
D.1
.
1
, from
\Psi\vartriangleright{\Delta}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!\mathbf{0}
it
follows that
|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}=0=|\Psi|
and
\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)=\emptyset
.
Therefore,
\Gamma\uplus\Delta=\Gamma
.
Let
\Theta=\Phi
: so,
\Theta\vartriangleright{\Gamma\uplus\Delta}\vdash t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}
with
|\Theta|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}
and

|\Theta|=|\Phi|=|\Phi|+|\Psi|
.

•

Application
,
i.e.

t=us
.
Then
t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}=u\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}s\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}
and necessarily

\Phi=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$@$}}}\hbox{\hskip 8.42938pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 28.92363pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 42.15056pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox to18.66656pt{\leaders{\vbox to3.44444pt{\vss\hbox{.}\vss\par}}{\vfill}\par}\hbox to0.0pt{ \vbox{\vskip 5.16663pt\hbox{\ignorespaces$\Phi_{1}$}\vskip 5.16663pt\par}\hss}}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$\Gamma_{1},x\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}_{1}\vdash u\!:\![{\mathsf{O}}\multimap{\mathsf{M}}]$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox to18.66656pt{\leaders{\vbox to3.44444pt{\vss\hbox{.}\vss\par}}{\vfill}\par}\hbox to0.0pt{ \vbox{\vskip 5.16663pt\hbox{\ignorespaces$\Phi_{2}$}\vskip 5.16663pt\par}\hss}}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$\Gamma_{2},x\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}_{2}\vdash s\!:\!{\mathsf{O}}$}}\par}\par}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma,x\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}\vdash us\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}$}}}\par}\par}}}$}

with
|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi_{1}|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Phi_{2}|_{\mathsf{m}}+1
,
|\Phi|=|\Phi_{1}|+|\Phi_{2}|+1
,
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\uplus\Gamma_{2}
and
{\mathsf{N}}={\mathsf{N}}_{2}\uplus{\mathsf{N}}_{2}
.
According to Lemma
D.1
.
2
applied to
\Psi
and to the decomposition
{\mathsf{N}}={\mathsf{N}}_{1}\uplus{\mathsf{N}}_{2}
, there are contexts
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
and derivations

\Psi_{1}\vartriangleright{\Delta_{1}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}_{1}
and

\Psi_{2}\vartriangleright{\Delta_{2}}\vdash v_{\mathsf{t}}\!:\!{\mathsf{N}}_{2}
such that
\Delta=\Delta_{1}\uplus\Delta_{2}
,
|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Psi_{1}|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Psi_{2}|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Psi|=|\Psi_{1}|+|\Psi_{2}|
.

By
i.h.
, there are derivations
\Theta_{1}\vartriangleright{\Gamma_{1}\uplus\Delta_{1}}\vdash u\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}\!:\![{\mathsf{O}}\multimap{\mathsf{M}}]
and
\Theta_{2}\vartriangleright{\Gamma_{2}\uplus\Delta_{2}}\vdash s\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}\!:\!{\mathsf{O}}
such that
|\Theta_{i}|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Phi_{i}|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Psi_{i}|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
|\Theta_{i}|\leq|\Phi_{i}|+|\Psi_{i}|
for all
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Since
\Gamma\uplus\Delta=\Gamma_{1}\uplus\Delta_{1}\uplus\Gamma_{2}\uplus\Delta_{2}
, we can
build the derivation

\Theta=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 11.0451pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hskip 3.00003pt\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\footnotesize$@$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 45.1933pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 58.42021pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox to18.66656pt{\leaders{\vbox to3.44444pt{\vss\hbox{.}\vss\par}}{\vfill}\par}\hbox to0.0pt{ \vbox{\vskip 5.16663pt\hbox{\ignorespaces$\Theta_{1}$}\vskip 5.16663pt\par}\hss}}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$\Gamma_{1}\uplus\Delta_{1}\vdash u\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}\!:\![{\mathsf{O}}\multimap{\mathsf{M}}]$}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$$}}\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox to18.66656pt{\leaders{\vbox to3.44444pt{\vss\hbox{.}\vss\par}}{\vfill}\par}\hbox to0.0pt{ \vbox{\vskip 5.16663pt\hbox{\ignorespaces$\Theta_{2}$}\vskip 5.16663pt\par}\hss}}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$\Gamma_{2}\uplus\Delta_{2}\vdash s\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}\!:\!{\mathsf{O}}$}}\par}\par}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma\uplus\Delta\vdash u\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}s\{x{\shortleftarrow}v_{\mathsf{t}}\}\!:\!{\mathsf{M}}$}}\par}\par}}}$}

where
|\Theta|_{\mathsf{m}}=|\Theta_{1}|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Theta_{2}|+1=|\Phi_{1}|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Psi_{1}|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Phi_{2}|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Psi_{2}|_{\mathsf{m}}+1=|\Phi|_{\mathsf{m}}+|\Psi|_{\mathsf{m}}
and
